## Assistant

Here's where we left off on the encapsulated-map-as-box issue, and what I think the next move is.

**Input side is done and on disk.** The most recent commit shipped load-time inlining: when the graph loader sees a sub-map referenced as a box, it slurps the sub-map's boxes into the parent's arena with prefixed ids, then rewrites every parent wire that targeted the encapsulating box so it goes *through* the matching externally-supplied data box and lands on whatever the data box was feeding inside the sub-map. There's an end-to-end fixture under `tests/maps/248-encap-input-only/` that proves bytes from the parent reach a write box inside the sub-map.

**Three things are still deferred** — listed in the issue's status header and now on the todo list:

1. **Output-side encapsulation.** Wires the user drew *from* the encapsulating box's output ports don't fire yet. The symmetric splice — every externally-consumed write box inside the sub-map surfaces as its own distinct output port on the parent's view of the encap box — is the natural next slice, since it mirrors the machinery the input side already proves out.
2. **Editor UI.** The inspector toggle (externally-supplied on data boxes, externally-consumed on write boxes) and the file-browser "Encapsulate" action that drops a `kind: "map"` box onto the parent at cursor position.
3. **Recursion** across nested encapsulations + cycle detection at load time.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The decision to inline at *load time* rather than compile time (path A in the issue) or with a true nested dispatch loop (path B) is a third path — splicing happens after the parent's JSON is read but before topology is resolved, so the runtime never sees `kind: "map"`. It gets path-A's flat-graph simplicity without depending on issue 309's compile pipeline being in shape.
- The reason encapsulated maps are the *one* documented exception to issue 218's "single output per box" rule is that each externally-consumed write box is genuinely an independent value with its own semantics — bundling them into a packed blob would have been file-write machinery doing wire-emit work, the wrong shape. Keeping the multi-output exception on this one kind preserves both invariants.
- Reference-counted artifacts (issue 315) and self-construction (319) are blockers for a real release because together they're what lets *other programs* hold onto a SoraMech artifact across time and let a running map mutate itself — until those land, SoraMech is single-user, single-shot.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

I also queued 319 and 315 on the todo list as you asked. Want me to dive into the **output-side splice** for 248 next? That's the smallest self-contained step that unblocks the rest of 248 (the editor toggle is mostly a UI affordance over data the loader already needs to read), and it'll let me show you a fixture where a wire leaving the encap box actually carries a value into the parent. Or would you rather I poke at 319 / 315 first since those are the release blockers?

## Assistant

Wrote `issues/250-routing-kind-nonlinearity.md`, mirroring the format from 240–243.

The shape of the issue: schema + branch-picker + inspector sections like its siblings, but with the recognition that **this is the first routing kind that rewrites the wire value** instead of just selecting a branch — which is the most interesting architectural note in there.

A few design notes I want to flag for your decision (all also expanded as open questions in the file):

- **Auto vs fixed bounds is the most interesting axis.** Auto means non-deterministic outputs across runs (a value early in the run remaps differently than the same value late in the run, once min/max have widened). That's a real cost — debugging downstreams becomes "what did the box see before this value?" One mitigation: a "freeze observed bounds" action that snapshots auto → fixed on save.
- **Sigmoid wants something to bend.** If you sigmoid a normalised `t ∈ [0,1]` directly, the output range is roughly `[0.5, 0.73]` — barely curved. You almost always want to center the input (`t - 0.5`) and apply a steepness `k`. So the "default sigmoid" needs a default `k`, and the question is whether to expose `k` to the user or pick something opinionated (I'd lean opinionated — `k ≈ 6` gives a satisfying S — and let power users edit the JSON directly).
- **Shape choice has implications for output range.** Sigmoid is `[0,1]`, tanh is `[-1,1]`. Either pick one and let `out_range` field map between them, or let the shape dictate. The first is more flexible, the second is less surprising.
- **The first value seen under `auto`** has no `[min, max]` yet. I picked "emit 0.5" as the bootstrap; alternatives are "emit input unchanged" or "suppress the emit" (breaks the "every fire emits one value" contract every other routing kind upholds).
- **Hard-error on non-numeric input** matches your preferences exactly — no silent coercion, no skip-on-bad-input fallback. `strtod` is "good enough" but accepts `"3.14abc"` as 3.14; strict parse (reject trailing junk) is the more consistent choice.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- Generalising the dispatch layer from "routing picks a branch" to "routing may also rewrite the value" is the architectural shift this issue introduces. If you ever want **square**, **log**, **abs**, or **quantise** as routing kinds, factor that path as a first-class concept now rather than special-casing this one kind.
- The auto-mode running min/max is the first slot kind in the project (that I'm aware of) where CAS-update semantics are load-bearing on shared state. Per-worker bounds would be faster but produce drifting output streams — shared with atomic CAS is the right shape but worth knowing it's the precedent it sets.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

**Which open question do you want to nail down first?** My read is the name (`nonlinearity` / `squash` / `remap`) and the bounds-mode default (`auto` vs `fixed`) are the two that have to settle before any code lands — everything else can be tuned after the first fixture runs.

The records are still alive (they migrated to the graph), but reading them through the freed *pointer array* is UB and crashed on the first dry run. Moving the `free` after the splice fixed it. The lesson is that "the records are still owned" doesn't mean "every container I held them in is still valid" — separate lifetimes.

## Assistant

Wrote `issues/320-box-reconfigure-via-third-input-ring.md`. The shape: a per-box auxiliary inbox carrying box-JSON-shaped reconfigure messages, treated as a scheduling peer to the per-port input rings, gated by a yield-and-requeue barrier instead of a spinlock.

A few design notes worth flagging for your decision (also expanded as open questions in the file):

- **Per-box vs per-port ring placement.** I went with per-box because the message concerns the whole box — but the user-facing phrase "third ring" could mean per-port (mirroring the native/JSON pairing exactly). If per-port, every port could carry config and "which port's config wins?" needs answering. Per-box is cleaner; flag if you'd rather mirror per-port symmetry.
- **Scheduler ordering between value-pops and reconfigure-pops.** Three defensible rules:
  - **Strict FIFO across all four channels** using issue 318a's per-cell ordering tags — most consistent with the "interleave naturally" model.
  - **Reconfigures get priority** — drain them before any fire; every fire runs under the latest config.
  - **Reconfigures get no priority** — values in flight keep their original interpretation under the old config.
  
  Option 1 matches your phrasing best ("placed in the queue the same as all the other inputs"); option 2 is the "configuration is always current" stance; option 3 is "values keep their original interpretation."
- **Yield-and-requeue target end.** You said both "end" and "beginning" in one sentence. End-of-queue is fairer (other tasks get to run before the contended worker retries); beginning is faster recovery. I'd default to end but it's your call.
- **Scope of reconfigurable fields in slice 1.** I proposed "cheap fields (lang/ref/fn/routing/output_capacity) + connections[]; reject changes to id/kind/inputs[] with a precise diagnostic; defer the expensive port-shape changes to a follow-on." Anything missing from that scope?
- **The "not-quite-a-spinlock" name.** I called it the "yield-and-requeue barrier" in the prose. It's a barrier in the sense that it's the gate, but cooperative-with-the-scheduler rather than blocking — your phrasing made me lean away from "lock" entirely. If you have a preferred name, the issue can adopt it.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The synchronization primitive you described is unusual in C runtimes and **interesting**: instead of a thread blocking when it can't make progress, it returns to the scheduler with a "retry me later" hint. This is the same shape Go's goroutine yield uses, and what `async/await` runtimes do when a future returns `Pending`. The cost is one extra trip through the scheduler; the benefit is no busy-wait, no priority inversion, no thread parking. For SoraMech's pool-based dispatch where every fire is a small unit of work, this is genuinely the right primitive — and naming it something other than "lock" (since it isn't one) matters for keeping the mental model honest.
- This issue is the symmetric counterpart to 319: 319 grows the graph (additive — `create_box`, `connect`), 320 mutates what's already there (replacing). Together they cover the full "graph as a living artifact" surface, which is what makes the runtime feel mutable in a coherent way rather than as a pile of escape hatches.
- The "JSON exactly matching the box's own schema" choice means **the box-file parser is doing double duty** — it parses the load-time JSON files AND the runtime reconfigure messages. One parser, one validator, one schema. If you ever change the box JSON shape, both paths get the change for free. That's the same economy of design as your `external` block being a single struct used by both ext-supplied data boxes and ext-consumed write boxes — one mechanism, two destinations.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Issue 320 rewritten and two memories saved (YARQ + third-ring vocabulary, and the no-tier scoping preference). 250 left untouched.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The per-port placement is conceptually stronger than per-box because it preserves the **single ordering scheme** that already governs value cells: if the producer pushes "value, value, reconfigure, value" onto a port, the consumer sees them in exactly that order. A per-box config inbox would have been a parallel scheduling channel, requiring its own ordering rules to interleave with port pops. Per-port means "one fewer thing to coordinate" — same scheduler, same FIFO, just one extra tag value.
- YARQ being **cooperative with the scheduler** rather than blocking is the move that unlocks per-cell reconfigure interleaving. A traditional lock would have meant "while reconfiguring, every other worker that touches this box stalls a thread"; YARQ means "while reconfiguring, every other worker that touches this box returns to the pool and gets used for other work." The barrier holder doesn't pay a coordination cost; the contended workers each pay one extra scheduler trip. That's a much friendlier accounting for a runtime where thousands of tiny tasks compete for tens of workers.
- The comprehensive-first-slice preference is the right call **because the cross-cutting plumbing IS the work**. Splitting "cheap fields now, port-shape changes later" would mean writing the input-gather → YARQ → reconfigure-apply pipeline twice (once for the easy case, once again with slot-store growth/shrink threaded through). Doing both in one pass means the plumbing is shaped correctly from the start — slot store calls live next to JSON parse calls live next to producer re-resolution calls, all in one function, one mental model.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Educational notes for 250

### Why an S-curve with a midpoint? (the math vision)

Your intuition is exactly right and exactly why neural networks use sigmoid/tanh as activation functions. Here's the underlying mechanism:

A sigmoid acts as a **soft decision boundary**. Values near the midpoint say "I have no opinion." Values far from the midpoint say "I'm confident in this direction." The shape captures the difference between *uncertainty* and *commitment* in a single function — and it does it smoothly so the gradient is continuous (which matters when you compose many of them).

When you **multiply two midpoint-0 outputs together**, you get a behavior that's structurally identical to **soft AND**:
- `0.9 × 0.9 = 0.81` (both strong positive → strong positive)
- `0.9 × -0.9 = -0.81` (disagreement → strong negative)
- `0.1 × 0.9 = 0.09` (one uncertain → uncertain)
- `0.05 × 0.05 = 0.0025` (both uncertain → very uncertain)

This is the **noise-rejection** behavior you sensed. Weak evidence doesn't accumulate into strong conclusions, because uncertainty multiplied by uncertainty stays uncertain. Strong evidence reinforces because confidence × confidence stays confident. Two boxes wired into a multiply box become a soft logic gate; chain them and you have a network of weighted votes that converges on a decision.

So your three variants map cleanly:

- **Midpoint at 0** (tanh-style, output `[-1, 1]`) — for "agree/disagree/abstain" semantics. Multiplication composes like soft AND with sign-tracking. **Best for decision-making agents** where signals have a polarity.
- **Midpoint at 0.5** (sigmoid-style, output `[0, 1]`) — for "probability/confidence" semantics. Multiplication composes like a probabilistic conjunction (assuming independence). **Best for evidence-accumulation pipelines** where everything is a likelihood.
- **No midpoint, just scaling** (linear, output `[0, 1]`) — for "calibrate this range to that range" with no decision content. **Best when you're just remapping a sensor reading** to a UI slider's range.

### How do you build intelligent behavior with this?

The mechanism is **composition**. Wire many nonlinearity boxes together, each looking at different inputs, each with its own bounds and steepness. The output of each is a confident "yes/no/abstain." Combine those outputs through multiplication (soft AND), addition followed by another nonlinearity (soft OR with re-thresholding), or weighted sums followed by a nonlinearity (the perceptron — the building block of deep learning).

An RL-style agent might use:
- Sensor box → nonlinearity (raw → confidence)
- Two confidences → multiply (soft AND)
- Many such AND-ed signals → weighted sum → another nonlinearity (decision threshold)
- Decision threshold → comparator routing → action

The reason this gets called "intelligent" is that the network of soft thresholds learns to ignore irrelevant signal combinations (they multiply to near-zero) and amplify relevant ones (they multiply to near-one), without anyone hand-coding which combinations matter.

**One question back to you**: are you building toward something more like a **neural-net-style perceptron lattice** (many sigmoids feeding weighted sums feeding more sigmoids), or more like a **bayesian/probabilistic pipeline** (sigmoids producing confidences that get conjuncted/disjuncted)? The choice changes what default shape / midpoint you'd want, because the two paradigms have different multiplication semantics.

### The dampening / equilibrium idea

This is a real technique — it's called **exponential moving average** of the bounds. Concretely:

```c
// every fire, decay both bounds slightly toward the midpoint
double mid = (min + max) / 2.0;
min = α * min + (1 - α) * mid;     // 0 < α < 1, e.g. α = 0.99
max = α * max + (1 - α) * mid;

// then if the new value extends the range, snap to it
if (x < min) min = x;
if (x > max) max = x;
```

Per-fire decay rate of `1 - α = 0.01` means the bounds halve their distance from the midpoint roughly every 70 fires. So an outlier from 1000 fires ago has decayed to ~0 influence by now, but a recent outlier still counts.

**Your minimum-gap question**: enforce it as a floor on `max - min`. If decay would bring them within `ε`, freeze decay until new values widen them. Or, equivalently: only decay when `max - min > 2ε`, so the gap never falls below `2ε`.

**Your re-calculation question**: yes, the curve gets re-computed every fire — but it's just `t = (x - min) / (max - min)`, three operations. Free. No precomputation needed.

**One question back to you**: do you want decay to be **symmetric** (both bounds pulled toward the midpoint equally) or **asymmetric** (the bound furthest from the midpoint decays faster, so a single outlier on one side decays quickly while the other side stays stable)? Asymmetric tends to feel more like "settling" because outliers don't bias the midpoint after they're gone.

### The midpoint-branch idea (your line 133-136)

Yes, this is a real and useful pattern — but I'd argue it's a **separate routing kind**, not a feature of nonlinearity. What you're describing is roughly: "comparator-with-magnitude" — split into above/below branches AND carry the magnitude through. Pros: captures direction+strength in one box, matches RL agent vocabulary. Cons: blends two concerns (which branch + transformation) and couples them.

The composition path is already clean: wire your nonlinearity to a downstream comparator. The nonlinearity gives the magnitude on the wire; the comparator splits the branches based on its midpoint. Two boxes, separate concerns, fully composable.

If you want it as one box, file it as a new kind — `directional-magnitude` or similar — rather than extending nonlinearity. Same reasoning as why the issue is for "value-transforming routing" specifically: keep each kind's job small and composable.

### The out-of-range "scaling value" idea (your line 298-301)

What you're describing sounds like **elastic bounds**: the user-supplied `min` / `max` are *initial estimates*, not hard limits. When a value exceeds them, the bounds widen to include it (same as `auto`), the curve re-computes, and the value gets normalized to its new position. No clamping, no erasure — the bounds just stretch.

This collapses cleanly into your fixed/auto-flag design:
- Both flags set (`fixed_min` AND `fixed_max`) → fixed mode, sigmoid saturates naturally on out-of-range (linear/softsign error out).
- Neither flag set → pure auto, bounds track the data.
- **One flag set** → asymmetric mode: the fixed side is a hard limit, the unset side tracks the data. Out-of-range on the fixed side is a hard error; out-of-range on the auto side just widens.

That last case is genuinely useful: "I know my values are always positive (fixed_min = 0), but I don't know how big they get (auto max)." Worth surfacing as a deliberate design point rather than an emergent property.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The reason sigmoid keeps showing up across so many different domains — neural networks, logistic regression, biological neuron models, dose-response curves in pharmacology, demand curves in economics — is that it's the simplest function that captures the abstract phenomenon of "smooth transition between two regimes." Anywhere you have soft thresholds, sigmoid is the natural fit.
- Your "AND of two sigmoids = soft AND" insight is essentially the discovery the perceptron paper (Rosenblatt, 1958) was built on. Deep learning is layers of weighted sums followed by nonlinearities. Three boxes of yours, properly wired, are one neuron. Many such triples wired into a lattice are a neural network.
- The fact that you arrived at "elastic bounds with a fixed/auto flag per side" without knowing the math is a sign the design's grain is correct — when the right primitive falls out of the requirements independently of the theory, you can usually trust the primitive.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Quick followups on 250

**Three variants by name, not by mathematician's jargon.** Yes, this is the right move — and it collapses two of your fields into one. Instead of `shape: sigmoid|tanh|linear` plus a separate `out_range: unit|signed`, just have `shape` carry an intent-named value:

- `shape: "decision"` → tanh under the hood, midpoint 0, output `[-1, 1]`. UI tooltip: "Soft + / − with clear neutral. Compose by multiplication when signals agree."
- `shape: "confidence"` → sigmoid under the hood, midpoint 0.5, output `[0, 1]`. UI tooltip: "Soft yes / no with uncertainty. Compose probabilistically."
- `shape: "calibration"` → linear remap, no midpoint, output `[0, 1]`. UI tooltip: "Pure range conversion, no curve. Use when you just want to rescale a sensor reading."

Three picks, three clear meanings. The user never has to know what tanh is.

**Symmetric vs asymmetric decay — your reframe is elegant and self-deciding.** Presence of a user-supplied midpoint IS the mode switch:
- Midpoint supplied → asymmetric (the midpoint anchors; bounds drift toward it).
- Midpoint absent → symmetric (the midpoint follows the data; both bounds drift toward each other).

No separate flag needed. The field's presence carries the meaning.

**Multi-band-comparator extension (+ button, absolute/percentage rows) belongs in 243, not 250.** The "+ button to add tiers" is a generalization of the multi-band-comparator UI, and the synced absolute/percentage rows are a UI convenience that works against any range — comparator OR nonlinearity OR anything else with bounds. If you want, I can write an update note onto 243 sketching it. The auto-min/max-tracking primitive that the percentage row reads from is the same primitive nonlinearity uses for `auto` mode — there's a small architectural insight there about factoring "tracked range" as a shared capability.

**Clamping as "certainty threshold" — yes, brilliant.** This reframes the user's `min`/`max` from "where the curve starts to saturate" into "the threshold past which I'm certain." That maps cleanly per variant:
- `confidence`: `input > max` → output = 1.0 (fully confident yes); `input < min` → output = 0.0 (fully confident no). Clamp away.
- `decision`: `input > max` → output = +1.0; `input < min` → output = −1.0. Clamp away.
- `calibration`: don't clamp (it's a remap, not a decision). Out-of-range values either extrapolate linearly or hard-error per your preference.

So clamping is a **per-variant default** rather than a separate toggle. Confidence and decision variants clamp; calibration doesn't.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The "auto-tracked range" primitive that nonlinearity's auto mode introduces is the kind of thing worth promoting to a first-class concept eventually. The multi-band-comparator's percentage row wants it; a histogram visualization box would want it; a normalization box (if you ever build one) wants it. Right now it lives inside nonlinearity, but once two boxes want the same thing, it earns its own ticket. Watch for that.
- Your reframe of "clamp = certainty threshold" is the kind of move that makes APIs feel natural. The user was probably going to clamp anyway because "values past my limits are obvious extremes"; by naming what they're already doing, the system gets a primitive that matches intuition without anyone having to memorize it.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

**On 320** — I checked. The current state already accommodates everything new from this conversation (steepness `k` reconfigurable, shape variant reconfigurable, decay flag reconfigurable, all covered by the "every reconfigurable field" comprehensive scope). No edit needed.

## Assistant

315 shipped. Five files changed:
- **`scripts/soramech-ref.sh`** (new) — the acquire/release/list/count/reap helper. Back pointer is PID + `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22 (start time in clock ticks). Optional marker file path as a second liveness signal. Append-only log; `mkdir`-style lock only on `reap`.
- **`scripts/soramech-compile.sh`** — consults the log via the new `count` action. Zero live refs → destructive rebuild on `compiled/` (the old behaviour, unchanged). One or more live refs → fork to the next `compiled.N` sibling. Forked manifest carries `forked_from` pointing back at the parent generation.
- **`.gitignore`** — extended with `tests/maps/*/compiled.*/` for the sibling pattern.
- **`tests/315-refs-test.sh`** (new) — 12 unit scenarios: fresh/acquire/release/count cycles, two-acquires partial-release, fabricated-dead-PID stale detection + reap, marker-file presence gating, JSON shape of `list`.
- **`scripts/run-tests.sh`** — folds the unit suite into the integration suite, plus an end-to-end fork check that compiles, acquires, compiles again, and asserts the pinned generation still runs.

Suite: **23 passed, 4 pre-existing failed** (all 319-series, unchanged). Both new 315 tests green; the existing `compile_pipeline_check` still passes alongside.

**Todo recap.** Three of five done (both 248 sub-tasks I could finish + 315). Two left:
- **#2 — 248 editor toggles + Encapsulate action.** JS/HTML in `assets/`. I can read the existing inspector + file-browser code and make changes, but verifying needs you in a browser with the dev server.
- **#4 — 319.** "Open — planning stub" with several design questions you'd need to resolve before code lands; better tackled in conversation than solo.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The `count` action I added to the ref helper (beyond the issue's spec of `list` / `acquire` / `release` / `reap`) is the load-bearing piece for the compile script's fork decision. Returning JSON from `list` is great for diagnostics but bad for shell logic — parsing JSON with grep is fragile, and the compile script's question is a yes-or-no. A separate integer-emitting action means the script's check is one `[[ "$live" -eq 0 ]]` comparison instead of a regex over a JSON blob. Small API addition; big simplification at the call site.
- The PID + start-time-ticks back pointer is the cheapest reliable "is this process still the same one" verification on Linux. The PID alone is a recycling hazard (kernels reuse PIDs in days); the start-time-ticks anchor pins the identity to a specific process incarnation. Match both → "yes it's the same holder"; mismatch → "the PID got reused by something else, treat this ref as stale." No kernel calls, no parent tracking, just `/proc/<pid>/stat` parsing.
- The "compile/ vs compile.N/" naming asymmetry (the first generation is `compiled/`, forks are `compiled.1/`, `compiled.2/`) is a deliberate divergence from the issue's "symlink + always-indexed" suggestion. The reason: single-user development never forks, so the canonical name should be the canonical path, not a symlink that resolves to `compiled.0/`. The fork path only adds artifacts when the system genuinely needs them. The cost is no "latest" pointer — callers have to track which generation they want — but in practice each caller already knows which generation they acquired against, so the pointer wouldn't have helped them.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The encap button on the map-picker row is the **single editor surface that closes the loop on issue 248**: without it, the only way to create an encap box was to hand-edit JSON. Hand-edited boxes work but bypass every UI safety net. With this button, the user can click a map, get an encap box pre-populated with the right port shape derived from the sub-map's own external markings, and just wire it up. The "derive ports from the sub-map's own marked boxes" step is what makes the affordance trustworthy — no double-entry, no risk of the parent's encap declaring ports the sub-map doesn't have.
- Loosening `from_branch` to accept any non-empty string was the schema change with the most reach. Before: the field was restricted to lt/eq/gt/`out_<n>` because every routing kind that used it had a fixed vocabulary. After: encapsulated maps can use it freely for named output ports. The lexical schema gives up some precision, but the precision moves to the graph-load step where it actually belongs — the loader knows the producer's kind and can verify the port name exists. The schema's job is shape, not semantics; this change honours that boundary.
- I deliberately didn't auto-clear `ref` / `fn` / `routing` when a box flips from `call` to `map` (or vice versa). The user can edit them manually afterward, and stale fields get ignored by the kind-appropriate loader path. The alternative — implicit field clearing on kind-change — risks discarding work the user wanted to preserve when they were just exploring. The "fields ignored when the kind doesn't read them" rule is a quieter and more forgiving contract than "fields wiped on transition," and it composes naturally with issue 320's reconfigure path where every field can change at runtime anyway.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

3. Selecting a `read` or `write` box in the inspector should show the new "externally-supplied / externally-consumed" toggle + binding controls.
4. Selecting a `map` box should show the specialized inspector with ref + inputs + outputs lists.

## Assistant

Suite green at 27 passed / 0 failed — a project-first as far as I can tell from the prior 4-fail baseline. Marked task #4 complete.

**Honest report on what happened with 319:**

The parent issue's `## Open design questions` is fully resolved — every Q (Q1, Q1.5, Q2, Q3, Q4, Q5) carries a **RESOLVED** marker with the chosen shape spelled out. The implementation work is also done in six completed sub-issues 319a–319f (slot store growth, dispatch cap removal, box id + compile cache, the create_box/connect builtins, C/Bash bindings, and the language-agnostic dispatch box-kinds).

The release-blocker status was about the **four failing integration tests** that segfaulted every time you ran them. So I dug in:

1. Reproduced reliably — 8/8 runs crashed with exit=139 after "run complete" prints (so the segfault was during teardown).
2. Traced `push_to_downstream` → the new connection IS visible to the trigger's fan-out with correct `to_box_idx=2, to_input_idx=0`.
3. Traced `push_one_connection` → `dst='auto_00000000' input_slot_ids=NULL → SKIP`. The new box's `input_slot_ids` field reads as NULL at the moment the trigger tries to push to it.
4. Traced `runtime_create_box` itself → `input_slot_ids=0x7f5fa80071d0`. The field was correctly populated when the box was created. So somewhere between create-time and push-time, the read was returning NULL.

Then I removed all the traces to restore the original code, rebuilt — and **the tests started passing 30/30 reliably**. Re-ran the full suite: 27/0.

**The 319 tests now pass, but I haven't actually fixed the source-level bug.** The only code diff from where I started this session is my 248 ext-consumed write-box dispatch change (which the 319 tests don't exercise). My best guess: the new branching in `do_write_box` shifted instruction layout enough to mask a latent timing-sensitive race in the runtime_create_box → push_one_connection sequence. Add a single line elsewhere in dispatch.c and the bug might come back.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The most suspicious code-level smell is in `push_one_connection`: `const box_t *dst = graph_box(...); if (!dst->input_slot_ids) return 0;` reads through a `const` pointer to a struct field that's *not* declared atomic. If the compiler is allowed to cache that field load across function calls (and `const` plus aggressive inlining can encourage that), it might be reading a stale NULL from when the box was first calloc'd, before the population loop ran. The proper fix would be to either make `input_slot_ids` `_Atomic`, or add an explicit `atomic_thread_fence(memory_order_acquire)` after the `graph_box` call. **Until that fence lands, the green status of these tests is build-fragile.**
- The pthread_mutex in `graph_add_box` is a full memory barrier on unlock, so cross-thread visibility is correct. The race lives in the SAME-thread case: trigger's worker creates the box, immediately publishes a connection to it, immediately fans out its return value to the connection — all on one thread. Same-thread visibility should be free, but the compiler's optimizer can defeat it through aggressive const-cache or inlining. This is the same class of bug as a `volatile` mistake from the 1990s; the modern answer is `_Atomic` typing, not "rely on the compiler being conservative."
- The fact that adding ANY fprintf made the tests pass is the textbook signature of this kind of bug. fprintf is a function call the compiler can't see through, so it acts as a compiler barrier, forcing the optimizer to write back caches and re-read fields after the call. That's why "add a printf and it works" is the universal C race-detection folklore.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

**Recommended follow-up issue** (not in this session): "319 — harden the same-thread visibility of runtime-created box fields." Add `_Atomic` to `input_slot_ids` and `n_inputs` on box_t, plus matching atomic accessors. The current green tests would still pass; the latent race would stop being a layout-dependent ghost.

- 319 (no code changes; tests started passing as side effect)

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The `git add -p` interactive-only constraint forced a different technique here: snapshot final → revert to HEAD → write intermediate → commit → restore → commit. It's mechanically heavier than a clean partial-staging but more reliable for mixed-purpose hunks, especially when the diff has the additions interleaved (the four new functions in `run-tests.sh` weren't grouped by issue). The snapshot lives in `/tmp/` for the duration; if anything goes wrong, you `cp` the snapshot back and try again. Worth keeping in mind for future multi-issue diffs.
- The commit messages followed the project's `disprefer referring to functions by name` rule from CLAUDE.md, so each message describes mechanics in english (`the dispatch's write-box behaviour grew a small branch`) rather than `do_write_box now checks b->external.kind`. The code is the source of truth for names; the commits are for the future reader skimming history.
- The memory updates aren't in this repo (they live under `~/.claude/projects/-mnt-mtwo-programs-sora-soramech/memory/`), so the strategem commit message *mentions* them but doesn't add files for them. Auto-memory persists across sessions on its own.

All three closed out cleanly. Three new commits, each preserving file history via `git mv`:

```

fbe5966 315 — closeout: reference-counted artifacts moves to completed
8e4ace9 248 — closeout: encapsulated-map-as-box moves to completed
```

Each closeout commit did three things: bumped the issue's status to `complete` with a precise description of what shipped (and what's a separable follow-up vs deferred-from-scope), updated the relevant `phase-X-progress.md` table row, and `git mv`'d the issue file into `completed/`. The 319 closeout also added a missing 319f sub-row to phase-3 since that one had shipped without being recorded.

Working tree is clean except for your pre-existing untracked WIP (247, 249, readme.md).

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The pattern your CLAUDE.md prescribes — `completion = status update + phase-progress + move + commit, all together` — is doing real work here. The phase-progress row is what makes the completed/ directory *queryable* without opening every file; the status update inside the file is what makes it *recoverable* (a future reader sees "complete" rather than wondering if the move was accidental); the `git mv` preserves blame/log history. Skipping any one of those three would have left a small future-you debugging task.
- The 315 closeout was the one where I had to be careful about "follow-on questions" vs "deferred work". The four follow-ons listed in 315's design (runner self-acquire, reap-all across generations, log versioning, editor extension) are *separable* — each could be its own one-issue feature. Marking 315 complete and noting them as future-issue candidates is honest. Marking 315 "in progress" would have left it open forever waiting for ideas that haven't been scoped yet. The distinction your CLAUDE.md draws is sharp: deferred-from-original-scope keeps the issue open; separable follow-ups don't.
- The 319 closeout captures something genuinely uncomfortable: the parent issue's *design* is complete and the *implementation* works (every test green), but there's a latent race in `push_one_connection` that the current build layout happens to hide. Writing that honestly in the status field (rather than hand-waving) means a future reader investigating a recurrence has the lead — they see "input_slot_ids visibility, layout-dependent" and don't have to re-discover it from scratch.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Assistant

Two new commits on top of the closeout series — the fixup that lands the missing "complete" status text into the three completed/ files, and the 311 instrumentation slice that gives 319's latent race a JSONL-visible signature. Working tree stays clean except for your pre-existing untracked WIP.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The earlier `git mv` bug is one of those silent traps: `git mv source dest` stages a rename *based on the file's last-committed content* and moves the working file. If you have unstaged modifications on `source` when you run `git mv`, the rename captures HEAD content and your modifications sit unstaged on the destination path. The right pattern is `git add source` (stage the modification) THEN `git mv source dest` — or, equivalently, `git mv source dest` then `git add dest` to stage the modification on top. The closeout commits committed clean renames with stale content; the fixup commit lands the actual intent. Worth remembering as a generic git-hygiene rule.
- The 319 latent race now has a **JSONL signature** rather than a fprintf-and-rebuild diagnosis loop. If it ever resurfaces, the transcript will literally show `{"event":"push", ..., "result":"input-slots-null"}` — no source code change needed to recognise it. That's exactly the kind of "make the bug visible from the log alone" payoff that justifies adding instrumentation events even when they don't drive a feature directly. The push-event design captures *every* skip reason as a distinct string, not just the one we care about, so other future races (`dst-null`, `to-input-out-of-range`) get the same diagnostic treatment for free.
- The split between **always-emit mutation events** (box_create, wire_add, runtime slot_alloc) and **opt-in per-push events** (`SORAMECH_LOG_SLOTS=1`) is the right verbosity boundary. Mutations are O(graph edits), which the user controls and which are load-bearing for any audit log. Pushes are O(every wire fire), which is unbounded — gating them behind an env var keeps default runs cheap while making deep diagnosis one env var away.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

311 stays in `issues/` (not moved to `completed/`) — its lock-free MPSC ring, JSONL normaliser, and phase-3 demo map are still open as recorded in the updated status block. If you want me to push toward closing any of those, or pick something else, say the word.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The demo design landed on **deriving its narration from the transcript** rather than hand-coding output. That means every new event type 311 adds is one grep-and-extract away from being part of the demo's story. If a future change adds `reconfigure` events (issue 320), the demo could grow a "reconfigured boxes" section in one block of the script. The transcript is the single source of truth; the demo is a viewer.
- Three tasks running on workers 0, 1, and 7 (per the JSONL `worker_idx` field) confirms the runtime-spawned boxes are genuinely parallel — they're not all queued behind one busy worker. The pool's task distribution treats freshly-created boxes as first-class, with no warm-up gap. That's a quiet correctness checkpoint the demo also passes implicitly.
- The `PHASES=3` bump in `demo.sh` with no `PHASE=2` branch is a deliberate gap, not an oversight. Phase 2's product is the editor — not a runner-style demo — so there's no obvious "demo" mode for it. The numeric ceiling is "highest phase that has a runnable demo," and skipping the middle reads more honestly than inventing a fake phase-2 demo just to fill the slot. The `else` clause's error message catches `./demo.sh 2` cleanly.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Phase 3 now has only two checklist items left on its goal sheet: "All 11 integration test maps pass" (5 of 11 named maps exist; the rest are gaps to fill incrementally) and "Phase 2 synchronous runner retired" (structural cleanup of `src/004-executor.lua` etc.). The 240 commit deliberately bundles the multi-kind editor surface (schema + canvas + inspector dropdown + per-kind controls + setters) since splitting interleaved JS hunks per-issue would create more friction than value; the 241/242/243 commits each carry just the fixture, the run-tests entry, the phase-2-progress row, and the move-to-completed for their own issue. 243 also carries its C-side work (the thresholds field + parsing + dispatch picker + MAX_BRANCH_NAME bump). `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The Make bug had been **silently corrupting struct reads for three or four sessions** every time we extended `box_t` or `routing_t`. Every "intermittent" 319 failure I saw, every "fixed by adding traces" mystery, every "clean rebuild made it work" — all the same root cause. Auto-header-deps is the kind of infrastructure that pays for itself the first time it prevents a wild-goose chase, and the diagnostic JSONL events from 311's earlier commit are what made it diagnosable at all (the push event's `result=to-input-out-of-range` was the breadcrumb that pointed at field-offset corruption rather than memory ordering).
- The `DIR := $(abspath ...)` immediate-assignment fix is the kind of make footgun that bites quietly: with `?=` recursive, `$(MAKEFILE_LIST)` changes every time `-include` pulls in another file, so `$(dir lastword ...)` silently mutates between expansions. The compile command's `-I/.../build/src/langs` (a path that doesn't exist) was the only visible symptom — and only when a header search actually missed. The dry-run `make -n` caught it immediately because it printed the actual cc command; without that the error message blamed lang-spec.h.
- Splitting four interleaved routing-kind issues into separate commits while the work was actually done in parallel needed pragmatic compromise. The infrastructure landed under whichever issue introduced the most schema/canvas/inspector machinery (240, since it added the multi-kind dropdown + n_outputs control + set_n_outputs extension all in one logical step). The other three issues' commits stay thin because their per-issue work is genuinely small — just a fixture, a test entry, and the closeout. "One commit per issue" as a rule scales when the work is per-issue separable; when it isn't, biasing toward "first-numbered issue carries the shared infrastructure" reads honestly in git log because the commit message says so.

## User

Can we make an issue file to make a box that's a timer? All it does is have a "tick-tock rate" that is either defined in the editor or given as an input value. The map shouldn't compile if there's no wires attached to this box AND there's no value defined in the editor. If there's a wire attached but no value has come in yet, it shouldn't run, but every time it runs it emits a single "true" boolean as it's output. When it receives a value, it should add itself to the thread pool queue as any other box, and then when it finishes it re-adds itself. If the timer value is ever set to 0, then when it re-adds itself it instead just stops. It can be restarted by adding another timer value as input, because when input slots are filled they are also added to the task queue as part of the normal operating procedure for any box.

how can we implement this without endlessly spinning with an otherwise empty queue, and without locking a thread in a sleep, and without a single timer thread running in the background?

## Assistant

Wrote `issues/251-timer-box-tick-tock-emitter.md` with the full design + open questions + suggested implementation steps.

Now your tough question directly: **how to wait between ticks without spinning, without locking a worker in sleep, and without a dedicated timer thread.**

My read of the design space — four options, with my pick at the end:

**Option A — extend the pool's idle-wait with a deadline.** The pool's worker threads already block on a condvar when no work is queued. Today that wait is "infinite until signalled by a new task." Change it to "wait until either new work arrives OR the next-scheduled-timer's deadline." A small priority queue inside the pool holds `(deadline, box_idx)` pairs; the idle worker uses `pthread_cond_timedwait` with the head's deadline as its timeout. On wake, drain expired deadlines into the work queue, then proceed normally.

**Option B — `timerfd_create` + `epoll`.** Each timer gets a kernel-managed file descriptor that becomes readable after its deadline. The pool's idle worker calls `epoll_wait`; kernel coalesces N timers into one wait. More machinery, but generalises to network / file boxes later.

**Option C — pure YARQ requeue at scheduler frequency.** The fire always re-queues itself immediately; on re-pull the worker checks if `now >= deadline` and YARQs back to the tail if not. **This violates your no-spinning constraint** — an otherwise-empty queue burns CPU round-robining a single non-ready timer task. Reject.

**Option D — same as A but with the priority queue inside the dispatch layer rather than the pool layer.** Functionally identical to A; the question is just which layer owns it. The pool owns the idle condvar, so the pool feels like the right home.

**My recommendation is A.** It satisfies every constraint cleanly:

- **No spin:** the worker is in a blocking system call (`pthread_cond_timedwait`).
- **No locked-in-sleep worker:** the worker doing the wait is *already* the idle worker the pool has when there's no queued work. You're not adding a new wait, you're tightening the timeout on the existing one. A new task arriving signals the condvar and wakes it just like today.
- **No dedicated timer thread:** whichever worker happens to be idle does it. If 14 workers are busy, none is doing the wait — the timer just fires when the next idle moment arrives, possibly slightly late. That graceful degradation is the right behaviour for a real-time pipeline that's overcommitted.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The subtle bit in your three constraints: **the pool already has an idle worker waiting on a condvar.** That's not "locking a thread in a sleep" in the sense you meant — it's the pool's correct idle behaviour. The trick is that we're not *adding* a wait; we're *parameterising* the wait the pool already does. The cost of the timer feature is zero additional threads, zero additional condvars, just a `+deadline` argument to one existing call site.
- A question back to you: **do you want the timer's first tick to fire at `now + rate_ms` (one-rate-of-wait before the first event) or at `now` (fire immediately on graph load, then tick every rate_ms after)?** Standard cron-style schedulers default to the first; "kick off a chain at startup" use cases want the second. I left it as an open question on the issue with a slight lean toward "fire at `now + rate_ms`" because that's what `setitimer` does, but either is defensible.
- Another question: **when a timer is still pending and the map quiesces around it** (no other tasks queued, all consumers done), does the run terminate or wait for the next tick? The natural answer for a long-running event pipeline is "wait" — the timer prevents quiescence. The natural answer for a test fixture is "terminate" — quiescence ignores pending timers. A `background: true` flag would let the user pick per timer, but adds schema surface. Lean toward "always prevents quiescence; introduce the flag if a test fixture asks for it."
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

The issue is sitting at `issues/251-timer-box-tick-tock-emitter.md`. Want me to draft any of the open-question answers more concretely, or pause for your design pass on it?

## Assistant

250 rewritten. The shape now:

- **Three intent-named variants** (`decision` / `confidence` / `calibration`) replace the old `shape`-by-mathematician-name + `out_range` split. Each carries its own midpoint, output range, and composition semantics. The user never has to know what tanh is.
- **Bounds-mode is decided by which sides the user pinned.** `min` + `max` both supplied → fixed; neither → auto; one supplied → asymmetric (that side fixed, other auto-tracked). Same presence-is-mode rule applies to `midpoint`.
- **Dampening as EMA toward the midpoint** for auto sides, with a minimum-gap floor; the supplied-midpoint case anchors the EMA target, the no-midpoint case lets the midpoint follow the data.
- **Clamping as "certainty threshold"** — your reframe. Per-variant defaults: confidence / decision clamp; calibration extrapolates (no certainty semantics).
- **Output is always a double**, and the wire carries the *response* (the smoothed [0,1] or [-1,1] number) rather than the scaled input. The user composes `input × response` in a downstream multiplier if they want.
- **Steepness `k`** as a sensible default + inspector knob + reconfigurable via 320. SVG preview with the middle-80% guides stays in scope (cute is justification too).
- **Per-box atomic min/max cells**, not per-worker or per-thread. Auto bounds non-deterministic across runs, by design; no debug output — wire a log box if you want the trajectory.
- **No persistence** across runs; user supplies fixed bounds if they want carry-over.
- **Strict string parsing** (no LLM-output number extraction — your own conclusion); boolean coercion handled per the C-style 1/0 convention with a fixture to lock it.

What's still open in the doc: the kind's name (`nonlinearity` / `squash` / `remap`), the default variant for new boxes, and the calibration variant's out-of-range mode (extrapolate vs hard-error vs clamp-to-1).

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The biggest design move here was your **"presence of a bound IS the mode for that side"** insight. It collapses three separate flags (`mode`, `fixed_min_flag`, `fixed_max_flag`) into a single JSON-shape rule: a field is either in the object or it isn't, and that's the answer. The C side then mirrors with two `is_fixed` booleans next to the two doubles — same information, two representations, both natural for their respective audiences. The schema becomes self-describing: a reader sees what's set and knows what mode the box is in without having to look up a flag's meaning.
- The decision to **emit the response, not the scaled input** is the call that makes this kind composable. If the nonlinearity scaled-and-multiplied internally, every consumer would get back a number whose meaning depended on the bounds the nonlinearity happened to be using. Emitting the response gives every consumer the same `[0, 1]` or `[-1, 1]` scale, regardless of what the nonlinearity is scoring against. Then "I want the scaled input" is a separate, optional composition the user opts into with a multiplier box. Same pattern as the `write` box keeping its `value` input separate from its `path` input — small box, single responsibility, the user wires together the pipeline they want.
- The doc preserves your dump-truck-and-raccoons line at the very bottom under "Notes on architecture." It's not just for flavour: that note is the design philosophy for what to do when a user holds the box wrong, and it belongs in the doc that someone reads when they're about to make exactly that choice for their own future kinds.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Assistant

250 shipped end-to-end in one commit:

```
1741621 250 — routing kind: nonlinearity (value-transforming, three intent-named variants)
```

What landed:
- **Schema** (`src/001-schema.lua`) — `nonlinearity` kind, three valid `shape` values, type-checks the optional `min` / `max` / `midpoint` / `k`.
- **C loader** (`src/010-graph-loader.h` + `.c`) — `ROUTING_NONLINEARITY` enum, `nonlinearity_shape_t`, eight new fields on `routing_t` including the per-bound `is_fixed` booleans and the two atomic running cells (uint64 bit-cast doubles), parser with sentinel-init (`+inf` / `-inf` for the running min / max so the first widen always succeeds).
- **C dispatch** (`src/012-dispatch.c`) — `ROUTING_NONLINEARITY` case in `push_routed`. Reads the input value, coerces booleans, CAS-loops widen on auto sides, computes the curve per variant, clamps under fixed mode for confidence / decision, extrapolates for calibration, EMA-dampens the auto bounds toward the midpoint each fire, emits the score as `%g`-formatted bytes on the single output port.
- **Editor** (`assets/js/004-inspector.js`) — `nonlinearity` dropdown option, default routing (`shape: 'confidence'`, `k: 1`), per-kind controls (variant dropdown with hover tooltips per variant; three bound inputs that show blank = auto-track; steepness input; footer note that the wire carries the score not the input), `set_nl_field` setter that applies the presence-is-mode rule (empty bound input deletes the field; number sets it).
- **Build** — `Makefile` gains `-lm` for the new `tanh` / `exp` calls.
- **Three fixtures** — one per variant, each pinning the input to a deterministic point of the curve and asserting the exact `%g` output: confidence at midpoint → `0.5`, decision at midpoint → `0`, calibration at 25% → `0.25`.

Suite 31 → 34 passed, no regressions.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The **uint64 bit-cast trick for atomic doubles** is the right move in C11 — `_Atomic double` works on most platforms but isn't strictly required to be lock-free, while `_Atomic uint64_t` is. The union-based bit-cast (`pun.d = score; atomic_store(cell, pun.u)`) gives us lock-free atomic widening without depending on a compiler-specific guarantee.
- The `*3` factor inside the sigmoid (`1 / (1 + exp(-k*t*3))`) is the small tuning constant that makes `k = 1` produce the doc-promised "middle 80% maps to [0.27, 0.73]" behaviour. Without it, raw sigmoid on `t ∈ [-1, 1]` is too gentle — the curve barely bends. The factor centres the action band at `k = 1`, which is what the user wanted as the forgiving default. A future `k` higher than 1 sharpens proportionally; `k = 2` gives the kind of soft AND that's useful for evidence accumulation.
- The **per-bound atomic widen + non-atomic EMA dampening** combo is deliberate. The widen has to be atomic because two concurrent fires could both see an out-of-range value and need to settle on the larger of the two. The dampening is the *opposite* direction (pulling toward the midpoint), so a concurrent race between two fires both writing slightly-decayed values is benign — both are pulling toward the same target, the later store wins, the bound stays well-behaved. Saving the CAS overhead on the common path (every fire dampens; only occasional fires widen) makes the kind cheap to run.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Okay how about this - what if we have a toggle that switches between 0,1 and -1,1 and then we keep the minimum and maximum values we've seen so far, slowly drifting toward the midpoint (0 or 0.5) asymmetrically by 10% each time. Never going above -1/0 or below 1. So if we receive -5 on the first iteration, and -10 on the second, each of those would evaluate to -1. But, if we get a third one that's -5 again, then it would evaluate to -0.5 because it's halfway between the minimum and the midpoint of the scope, which is -1,1 in this example. Actually, what if we have a "memory" value instead of drifting? This memory value is "how many mins/maxes to remember" and it's just an array that stores the previous values. Probably a ring buffer I guess... Anyway the min/max is set to the min/max of the ring-buffer, and usually we're just appending (or overwriting the oldest value) but if we ever overwrite a value that is exactly the same as the min/max, then we trigger a min/max update where we pull the min/max closer to the scope. We'd essentially just set the min/max to the min/max value of the memory. We should be able to store both of them in the same ring-buffer because, well, the minimum and maximum values are both on either side of the scope, so they shouldn't interfere with one another. That feels more... discrete? to me? than the percentage drift. Plus it enables users to use integers more, though since we're outputting a value between -1 and 1 or between 0 and 1, that's already not an integer, so idk... Ah! I can't tell what design would work best because I don't know what use-cases we have~! but I do know what's easier / harder on the users, and having semi-opaque names like "decision" or "calibration" might confuse them even more. Especially if they have a math background.

## Assistant

Pause-with-you mode rather than implement mode. Let me walk through what your proposal actually computes, then surface the question I think is load-bearing.

**Your proposal, distilled:**

- Output range: a toggle between `[0, 1]` and `[-1, 1]` (one design dimension instead of three opaque variants)
- Bounds: ring buffer of the last N observed values; `min = min(buffer)`, `max = max(buffer)`
- Output: linear remap from the data bounds to the scope range
- (No sigmoid, no tanh, no `k`, no drift — all gone)

What you've just described is essentially **automatic gain control with a sliding window** — same shape audio compressors use. It's a *clean* design and significantly simpler than what's currently in the tree. The math is one division per fire and the parameter surface is two knobs: range and buffer size. A math user reads it and immediately knows what it does; a non-math user reads "normalize my values to 0–1 over the last 100 samples" and also immediately knows what it does. Your naming concern goes away because there's no opaque variant — there's only one operation.

**What's lost** if we drop the S-curves entirely: the soft-AND composition property. Two `confidence` values near 0.5 multiplied together stay near 0.25 (uncertainty stays uncertain); two near 1.0 stay near 0.95 (confidence reinforces). That's the "noise rejection in evidence-accumulation pipelines" thing I wrote up. It's a real property of sigmoid, and it's the only thing that distinguishes this kind from "scale a number to a range." If you never plan to compose normalized scores by multiplication into downstream perceptron-like networks, that property is **moot** — and the simpler linear box is strictly better.

**Ring buffer vs EMA decay:** they're roughly equivalent for matching N and α. Ring buffer is more intuitive ("I remember the last 100 values"); EMA is cheaper (two doubles instead of N) and produces smoother bounds. Your "discrete is more honest" preference is aesthetically defensible and I don't think it's the load-bearing call.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The reason you keep circling on this design is that you're trying to pick a shape without knowing the use-case. That's the right instinct to notice. A design that's general enough to fit every imagined use-case usually fits none of them well, because the trade-offs go in different directions for different use-cases.
- "Decision" and "calibration" were my translation attempt — bridging math to intent. But your point about math-background users is sharp: they don't recognize "decision" but would recognize "sigmoid" instantly. A bridge that confuses both directions is worse than picking one audience and naming for them.
- Linear AGC ≠ less powerful. It's *differently* powerful. The S-curve gives you smooth saturation; AGC gives you adaptive scaling. Most "I want a number between 0 and 1" use cases want AGC. The S-curve use cases are specifically about compositional logic with downstream multipliers.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

**The question I want to ask back** before any code changes:

**What's the smallest concrete use-case where you'd actually wire one of these into a map?** If you can sketch one example — "I have a sensor reading that comes in at unpredictable scale and I want a slider to land between 0 and 1" or "I'm building a perceptron-like decision lattice where multiple confidence scores feed an aggregator that picks an action" — the design falls out of it. The first asks for linear AGC; the second asks for sigmoid. *Both* asks for both. No examples in mind asks for the simplest thing that could work.

Three paths I see, ordered most-to-least conservative:

1. **Rip out what's there, replace with linear-only + range toggle + ring-buffer bounds.** Simplest. Drop "nonlinearity" as the kind name — call it `normalize` or `scale` or `auto-range`. The S-curve story becomes a separate routing kind issue if a use-case ever appears, and the math name (`sigmoid` / `tanh`) is the right name for that future kind because its audience is math-aware.
2. **Keep what shipped, add the simpler linear-AGC alongside as its own kind.** Two routing kinds. The user picks by intent: "I want soft logic composition" → nonlinearity; "I want adaptive scaling" → normalize. Doubles the surface but covers both audiences honestly.
3. **Refactor the shipped one into a single curve-dropdown.** `shape` becomes `linear` / `sigmoid` / `tanh` (math names, for math users). `range` becomes a separate toggle. Bounds-tracking gets the ring-buffer treatment. This keeps everything but renames the variants and changes the bounds mechanism.

If you can answer the use-case question I'd guess path 1 or 2; if you can't, I'd lean path 1 just because it's the cheapest mistake to fix later.

Want to give me a use-case sketch, or should I leave this for you to mull and pick up another thread?

## Assistant

Refactor shipped. The commit chain reads honestly: 250 → "first attempt as designed earlier"; 253 → "refined after use-case clarified."

**What changed in 253 vs 250:**

| Dimension | 250 (shipped first) | 253 (now) |
|---|---|---|
| User-facing variants | `decision` / `confidence` / `calibration` | `range`: `signed` or `unit` |
| Bounds tracking | Fixed-pin OR per-bound EMA-decayed atomic cells | Per-box ring buffer of last N values |
| Bounds API | `min` / `max` / `midpoint` fields (presence = mode) | `memory` (positive int) |
| Output | Score alone (in [-1,1] or [0,1]) | `v × score` (gated linear unit) |
| Cold start | Emit variant-neutral value | Emit `v × neutral` (so first fire passes some signal) |
| Out-of-range | Clamp per variant (confidence/decision) or extrapolate (calibration) | Auto-extended; new extreme widens the buffer on the same fire |
| Concurrency | Atomic uint64 bit-cast doubles, CAS-loop widen, plain store EMA | Per-box pthread_mutex on the ring buffer |

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The **gated output** (`v × score`) is the move that makes this composable for decision lattices. When score ∈ [-1, 1] and you multiply N of them through a downstream aggregator, the midpoint-near values multiply toward zero (suppress) and the bound-near values pass through near full amplitude (vote with strength). Without the gating, the user would always need to insert a multiplier box downstream to recover the input — and forget to half the time. Building the multiplication into the box matches the actual use pattern.
- The **ring buffer is more honest than EMA** for "remember the last N observed values" semantics. EMA approximates that with one number that drifts; the buffer literally stores the N. A user who wants to debug "what's the current min/max?" can in principle peek the buffer (a follow-up feature) and see the actual values. With EMA, the bound is a smoothed approximation that doesn't correspond to any specific historical event.
- The 250 → 253 trajectory is a small case study in **why iteration is cheaper than perfection**. The first 250 commit took the design as I understood it at the time; the use-case conversation revealed three substantive shape problems; the 253 commit fixes them. Each piece — schema, loader, dispatch, editor, fixtures — was small enough to refactor in place. If 250 had shipped as a months-long monolith there'd be no room to course-correct; shipping it as a small commit means the second pass is also a small commit.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The runner finding **a longstanding `307-c-spec-test::compile_with_cflags` failure that the integration sweep had never surfaced** is the load-bearing payoff of 252. The integration tests catch end-to-end behaviour; the unit tests catch lower-level surface that integration doesn't necessarily exercise. Without a way to actually *run* the unit suite without paying for a full rebuild, that failure sat in the C spec for who-knows-how-long. The runner makes that gap visible the first time you use it.
- Parsing the trailing `N passed, M failed` line is a **convention** the unit tests already followed without anyone writing it down. The runner formalises that convention as the parse target — which means new test files have to follow it too, or their results get synthesised from exit-code alone (run-one's fallback path). Worth a brief note in `docs/006-test-coverage-map.md` if anyone trips on it.
- Captures landing in `tmp/` (RAM-backed via the project's symlink convention from CLAUDE.md) means the runner can re-print the verbose output for a failing binary without re-running it — useful when a binary is slow or non-deterministic. The cost is zero disk pressure and the captures vanish on reboot, which is the right shape for "this is debug noise, not a record."
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Assistant

Found it. The two `.so` files have **different checksums** (so the build IS producing different output), but `dlopen` returns the OFF version anyway. That's glibc's well-known same-path cache — even after `dlclose` drops the ref count to zero, `dlopen` of the same path can return the previously-loaded library rather than reading the replaced file from disk. The test exercises a path-reuse scenario that production doesn't hit (real boxes have unique `.so` paths). Fix: use a distinct path per compile in the test, document the limitation. Removing my debug traces first.

Two commits land:

```
be7ef4d docs: rewrite as a user-facing guide
ec274f9 tests/307: fix cflags test by giving each build its own .so path
```

**Docs now:**
- `000-table-of-contents.md` — restructured with "Start here" reading order
- `001-overview.md` — what SoraMech is + quickstart with a real two-box example
- `002-map-model.md` — boxes, wires, all seven routing kinds, encapsulation
- `003-editor.md` — canvas UI walkthrough + per-kind inspector + map picker + compile button
- `004-runtime.md` — runtime mechanics, JSONL transcript, compile pipeline, refcounted artifacts, parallelism
- `005-writing-boxes.md` — Lua / C / Bash quickstarts + runtime self-construction per language
- `006-test-coverage-map.md` — kept as-is

**307 fix:** distinct `.so` paths per build, stamped with both PID + timestamp; mirrors the production discipline of unique-per-box artifact paths. 179/179 unit, 33/33 integration, three consecutive 307 runs stable.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The 307 failure was a **test bug, not a runtime bug**. The C spec was correctly passing cflags through to gcc, the build was producing genuinely different `.so` files on disk, but `dlopen`'s same-path cache kept returning the previously-loaded mapping. Production code dodges this by deriving `.so` paths from box ids (each box gets a unique path), so the cache never returns wrong content. The test was the only place where two builds raced for one path. Fixing the test rather than the spec was the right call — the spec's behaviour is correct under the assumption production gives it, and changing the spec to defensively re-`dlopen` on every invoke would slow every dispatch for a hazard no real workflow hits.
- The docs rewrite **deleted 5 files totalling ~1500 lines and replaced them with 5 files totalling ~750 lines**. The compression isn't because content was redundant — it's because the old docs were oriented around the project's development timeline (what each phase added, how features evolved). A user reading "Phase 2 used driver scripts; Phase 3 uses language specs" has to mentally compute "so what do *I* use?" The new docs answer that directly. The development-history information lives more honestly in the `issues/phase-*-progress.md` files where someone actually researching how the project got here would look.
- The big risk in this kind of doc rewrite is **losing institutional context** — the "structural shell" / apartment-building framing was a load-bearing way to think about why the runtime is shaped the way it is. I preserved a compressed version of it in the overview rather than throwing it out, because it answers the "why is X someone-else's-problem" questions that any user eventually hits. The full philosophy can live in `notes/vision`, which the ToC still points at.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Working tree leaves your inline 320 edits and the pre-existing untracked WIP (247, 249, 251, readme.md) where they were. Suite green: 179/179 unit + 33/33 integration.

4. Errors and fixes:
   
   - **319 visibility race** — Initial diagnosis was wrong (thought it was memory ordering). Actually a partial-rebuild trap: Make didn't track header dependencies, so changing box_t fields didn't rebuild all .o files. **Fix**: added `-MMD -MP` + `-include $(wildcard ...)` to Makefile, plus `DIR := $(abspath ...)` immediate assignment (was `?=` which got re-evaluated as MAKEFILE_LIST changed via -include).
   
   - **Make submake breaking after -MMD addition** — `DIR ?=` was recursive; after `-include` of .d files, $(MAKEFILE_LIST) included those, causing $(DIR) to evaluate to build/src/. Fix: changed to `DIR :=` immediate assignment.
   
   - **307-c-spec-test::compile_with_cflags failure** — glibc dlopen caches loaded libraries by absolute path even after dlclose. Test was compiling twice to same .so path. **Fix**: distinct .so paths per build (one ending "-default.so", one "-flagged.so", plus timestamp + pid). User explicitly requested: "change the path again, then run 307 specifically before doing the whole suite". I changed the path scheme to include timestamp+pid+suffix, ran 307 three times consecutively (13/13 each), then full suite (179/179 unit + 33/33 integration).
   
   - **Test binary link errors after 311 instrumentation** — 010-graph-loader-test couldn't find event_queue_wire_add symbols. **Fix**: added 013-jsonl-events.o and 014-event-queue.o to SPEC_LOADER_DEPS and 010-graph-loader-test deps in Makefile.
   
   - **Dangling sub_boxes pointer in encapsulation splice** — `free(sub_boxes)` was called before my new output-side splice tried to index into it. **Fix**: moved free() to after the output-side splice.
   
   - **Stale "complete" status text in closeout commits** — `git mv` of issue files captured HEAD content (in-progress text), not the working-tree edits I had made. **Fix**: separate "issues: land the status-complete text" commit (7b29b7f).
   
   - **User feedback on 248 commit shape** — User said "Also 315 and 319 aren't [in completed], but I thought we made git commits for them?... let's finish them up and re-do the commits." Created closeout commits 8e4ace9, fbe5966, c5f543d.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The **one-pop-per-cycle invariant** has bigger consequences than just "the issue's mixed-stream example was wrong." It collapses the whole design: a worker can't be mid-gather and discover a reconfigure on a later port, because each cycle visits each port exactly once with no re-entry. That means reconfigure can be applied as a **pre-gather sweep** with no retained values, no name-correspondence rebind, and no two-phase commit on the inputs array. The original design was twenty-step; the audited design is three-step.
- **YARQ is a mutator-side primitive only.** Workers reading the box record don't take it. The clean reading of the user's note is: YARQ protects against two mutators racing each other (reconfigure vs. reconfigure, or reconfigure vs. runtime_connect). Readers get cheap atomic loads. This is exactly the asymmetry that RCU-style structures lean on — many readers, occasional writers, writers serialise themselves but never block readers.
- **Reader consistency is the real design fork.** Today's `connections` array is append-only, so the publish order "pointer first, count second" + reader order "count first, pointer second" guarantees a safe snapshot. Reconfigure breaks append-only — inputs can shrink, rename, reorder. The three options I surfaced (atomic-swap bundle / generation counter retry / readers take YARQ) trade off restructure depth against per-read cost. I've left this open in the issue file because it's exactly the kind of decision that wants user input rather than my guess.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Audit's now reflected in the issue file. Two design decisions for you before any code lands:

**1. Where to start — 311 or 320?**
311's two leftovers (MPSC ring + JSONL normaliser) are small, self-contained, and would benefit 320 (because 320 adds at least one new event type). 320 is the big one. My recommendation: 311's leftovers first as warm-up + infrastructure, then 320. Tell me if you'd rather flip it.

**2. Reader-consistency shape for 320** — three options I sketched in the issue's new "Reader consistency under mutation — design knob" subsection:

- **A. Atomic-swap bundle struct** — clean, contention-free, restructures box_t. Cleanest snapshot semantics.
- **B. Generation-counter retry** — minimal restructure, small read overhead, retries are rare.
- **C. Workers acquire YARQ on reads** — simplest mechanically, but contradicts your "workers don't claim YARQ" note.

I'd rank these A > B > C. A matches the existing copy-and-publish discipline (just one level up — bundle the whole input shape, not just the connections array), and the per-read cost is one atomic load. Your call?

● **Learn by Doing**

**Context:** This is the foundational design decision for 320 — every other piece of the implementation depends on the reader-consistency shape. A is what I'd pick, but the choice has tradeoffs you might weigh differently (e.g., you might value keeping box_t's current layout flat over the cleanest snapshot semantics; or you might find generation counters more familiar from other systems). Whichever option you pick, the implementation pattern propagates everywhere else: `reconfigure_apply` lives or dies by it, the dispatch's pre-gather sweep code reads it, and runtime_connect's existing copy-and-publish either folds in (A) or stays separate (B / C).

**Your Task:** Reply with which of A / B / C to use, and any rationale you want recorded in the issue file. `★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **Vyukov MPSC vs. mutex-list, on this codebase:** the mutex queue wasn't a correctness problem — it worked perfectly. It was a *future* contention problem that only shows up under bursty dispatch (every task fires 3+ events; under 16-worker contention each event went through one append-mutex). The ring isn't faster on average, but its tail latency is bounded — no producer ever waits for another producer holding the mutex during slow JSON encoding. The interesting design choice was the full-ring backpressure (block on `has_space` CV) vs. drop-and-warn. I chose block because the JSONL log exists for audit trails — a dropped event is a hole in the timeline that defeats the whole point.
- **The normaliser's "key-sort + line-sort" trick:** if you sort just the lines, a re-ordered field in a single JSON object still diffs. If you sort just the keys per-object, a re-ordered event sequence still diffs. The combination kills both axes of nondeterminism. The same trick is what JSON canonicalisation (RFC 8785) does for cryptographic purposes — different reason, same shape.
- **n_workers stripped:** machine-portable expected files require us to drop the worker count because it defaults to CPU count. The audit trade-off: a test that genuinely depends on worker-count behaviour (e.g. 318's per-worker lang state) has to pin `SORAMECH_WORKERS` *and* read the un-normalised log. The fixture file is a "what is the same across machines" snapshot; per-machine behaviour belongs in the env or in a dedicated check.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`
