## Assistant

Yeah, you're right — I packed too much into the design knob and not enough explanation of what's actually moving through memory. Let me rewind and walk through it from the dataflow side.

## The setup, slowly

Picture a single box record sitting in memory. It has a bunch of fields hanging off it that the dispatch needs every time the box fires:
- A **list of input ports** — names like "x", "y", "z" with types.
- A **list of slot IDs** — one per input port, pointing at the slot where values for that port arrive.
- The **count** of how many input ports there are.
- A few other per-port arrays — the "is this port pop or peek?" modes, the "is this port fed by a same-language wire?" flags, etc.

All of these arrays are **the same length**, and they all change together. If you add a port, you grow all five arrays by one entry. If you rename a port, you change one entry in the names array but the slot ID stays put. They're a tight little cluster of stuff that describes "what this box's input shape looks like right now."

Now picture **two threads** trying to talk to this box at the same time:

- **Worker A** is mid-fire. It's reading the port list to figure out what inputs to gather. It looks at `n_inputs`, then walks through `inputs[0]`, `inputs[1]`, etc., pulling values from the corresponding slots.

- **Worker B** has just received a reconfigure message and is about to change the box's port shape. It needs to swap in a new port list — maybe one with three ports instead of two, maybe with port "x" renamed to "x_renamed", maybe with port "y" removed entirely.

## What can go wrong

If Worker B writes the new fields one at a time, and Worker A happens to be reading at the same instant, Worker A can see a mid-flight state that never existed in any coherent version of the box.

Concretely, imagine B writes the new `n_inputs` first, then the new `inputs[]` pointer. A might race in and see:
- New count: 3
- Old pointer (still pointing at the 2-entry array)

Now A walks the array thinking there are 3 entries when there are only 2. The third "entry" is memory past the end of the array — garbage. A fires the box with garbage inputs, or segfaults reading past the array.

Or imagine B writes the new pointer first, then the new count. A might see:
- New pointer (3-entry array)
- Old count: 2

That's actually safe! A processes 2 entries, ignores the third, fires the box with the first 2 inputs of the new shape. The reconfigure "half lands" but A doesn't crash.

So **the order of writes matters**, and one direction is safer than the other.

## Why the existing pattern works for the connections array

The wires-going-out array (the `connections` field) has the same race story, and 319d already solved it by carefully picking the write order:

1. Allocate a brand new array, copy the old entries, append the new entry.
2. Atomically publish the new pointer.
3. Atomically publish the new count.

And readers do the opposite: read count first, then pointer. If count is the new value, the pointer is guaranteed to also be the new value (because writer's pointer-store happened before writer's count-store). If count is the old value, it doesn't matter what the pointer is — readers using the old count only touch entries 0..(old_count - 1), which exist in both arrays.

**Why this works specifically for connections**: connections is **append-only**. The new array's first N entries are byte-identical to the old array's N entries. The new entries land at positions N, N+1, ... which old readers never look at. So even a reader holding the new pointer with the old count is reading completely valid data — just a subset.

It's an **apartment building where you can only add floors on top**. A visitor with an out-of-date map showing 10 floors can still navigate floors 1-10 just fine, because nobody renumbered them. The new 11th floor exists but visitors with old maps never go there.

## Why this trick **stops working** for reconfigure

Reconfigure isn't append-only. It can:
- **Remove** a port (the array shrinks).
- **Rename** a port at the same position (the array changes in place).
- **Reorder** ports (port "x" used to be at position 0, now it's at position 2).
- **Insert** a port in the middle.

So now the apartment-building analogy breaks. After a reconfigure, the floors got rearranged. Floor 3 in the old building isn't floor 3 in the new building — it might be floor 5 now, or it might not exist at all. A visitor with the old map who reads "go to floor 3" using the new building's directory ends up at the wrong place.

In code: a reader with old count = 5 and new pointer (pointing at a 3-entry array) walks entries 0..4 of an array that only has 3 valid entries. Entries 3 and 4 are past the end → segfault, or worse, silently reading whatever happened to be next to that allocation in memory.

So **the staggered-publish trick we use for connections doesn't carry over**. We need something else.

## The three options, explained as dataflow

Here are A, B, C again, but now told as "what happens at the level of memory accesses, step by step."

---

### Option A — Bundle everything that changes together into one swappable snapshot

**The shape:** Instead of `n_inputs` + `inputs[]` + `input_slot_ids[]` + `input_slot_modes[]` + … all being separate fields hanging off the box record, collect them into one struct (call it the box's **port configuration**) and store just one pointer to it on the box.

```
box.port_config → { n_inputs: 3,
                    inputs: [name1, name2, name3],
                    slot_ids: [5, 7, 9],
                    modes: [POP, PEEK, POP],
                    ... }
```

**What the mutator does:**
1. Allocate a brand-new port configuration struct.
2. Fill in all its fields with the new values.
3. Atomically swap the `box.port_config` pointer to point at the new struct.
4. Park the old struct on a free-later list (because some reader might still be holding the old pointer; can't free immediately).

That's it. The entire reconfigure is **one pointer swap**. Either readers see the old config or they see the new config — never a torn mix, because the writer never modifies the live struct, only swaps in a fresh one.

**What a reader does:**
1. Read `box.port_config` ONCE, save the pointer in a local variable.
2. Use that local pointer for the entire fire. Don't re-read it mid-fire.
3. When the fire is done, drop the reference (so the free-later list knows it's safe to reclaim).

Because the reader grabs a consistent snapshot at the start, it doesn't matter if the mutator swaps in a new config halfway through the reader's fire — the reader is using its local pointer to the OLD config, finishes its work cleanly, and the next fire of the same box will use the new config.

**Dataflow picture:**
```
Mutator side:          Reader side:
  build new config       grab pointer to current config (one load)
  swap pointer    ────►  ...keep using its snapshot through the whole fire...
  (old config drifts     drop reference at end
   on free-later list)
```

**What it costs us:** A medium-sized restructure of the box record. Today `n_inputs`, `inputs[]`, `input_slot_ids[]`, etc. are all separate fields directly on the box. To do option A, we'd lift them into a sub-struct and add the swap discipline. The dispatch code that reads them changes from `box->n_inputs` to `box->port_config->n_inputs`. Once-per-fire, one extra pointer dereference. Cheap to do, but it touches many call sites.

---

### Option B — A revision number, with readers retrying on mismatch

**The shape:** Leave the box fields where they are. Add ONE new field: `version` (just an integer). Every time the mutator changes anything about the inputs, it bumps `version` by 1.

The trick is the mutator bumps `version` to an ODD number BEFORE making changes, makes the changes, then bumps it to an EVEN number AFTER. Odd means "I'm in the middle of changing things, my data is torn." Even means "I'm done, the data is coherent."

**What the mutator does:**
1. Bump `version` from 4 to 5. (Now odd → "I'm working.")
2. Rewrite the `n_inputs` field, the `inputs[]` pointer, the `slot_ids[]` pointer, etc., one at a time.
3. Bump `version` from 5 to 6. (Now even → "I'm done.")

**What a reader does:**
1. Read `version` into a local variable, call it `v1`.
2. If `v1` is odd, the mutator is mid-write. Wait a tiny bit and retry.
3. Read all the fields the reader needs (`n_inputs`, `inputs[]`, etc.) into local copies.
4. Read `version` again, call it `v2`.
5. If `v1 != v2`, the mutator changed something while the reader was reading → the local copies might be torn. **Throw them away and go back to step 1.**

**Dataflow picture:**
```
Mutator side:                  Reader side:
  version: 4 → 5  (odd, "go    read version = 4
                  away")        read n_inputs = 5
  rewrite n_inputs               read inputs[] pointer
  rewrite inputs[] pointer       read version again = 4? then OK, use the data
  rewrite slot_ids[] pointer            = 5? throw out, retry
  version: 5 → 6  (even, "ok")          = 6? throw out, retry
```

**What it costs us:** Minimal restructure — just add the `version` field. But every reader now does the read-data-read-version-check dance. And on contention, readers retry, which wastes work. Also, the "odd/even" trick relies on careful ordering — get the bump-before vs. bump-after wrong and you reintroduce the torn-read problem.

There's also a subtler problem with this approach: option B assumes the reader can detect a torn read after the fact and retry. But if the torn read causes the reader to crash mid-walk (e.g., walking past the end of a shrunken array and segfaulting), there's nothing to retry. So we'd need to be very careful that mid-flight states are safe to *attempt* to walk, even if we throw the result away. That's a stronger constraint than it sounds.

---

### Option C — Make every reader wait on the YARQ barrier

**The shape:** Just lock. When the mutator wants to change things, it takes the YARQ barrier. When a reader wants to read things, it also takes the YARQ barrier. Only one party can hold it at a time.

**What the mutator does:**
1. Take YARQ. (If a reader holds it, wait.)
2. Rewrite the fields in any order. Nobody else is looking.
3. Release YARQ.

**What a reader does:**
1. Take YARQ. (If the mutator holds it, do the yield-and-requeue dance — drop yourself back at the end of the pool queue.)
2. Read whatever you need.
3. Release YARQ.

**Dataflow picture:**
```
Mutator side:                  Reader side:
  take YARQ ──────────────►    (blocked, waiting for YARQ)
  rewrite fields                ...
  release YARQ ◄──────────────  take YARQ (now succeeds)
                                read fields
                                release YARQ
```

**What it costs us:** Simplest mechanism, but it goes against the "workers don't take YARQ" note you wrote. Every single box fire pays for a CAS-acquire and CAS-release, even though the mutator path runs maybe once a minute and the read path runs thousands of times a second. Most of those reads are paying a sync cost for a race that almost never happens.

---

## How they compare in dataflow terms

The thing I want you to notice is that **A and B are doing essentially the same thing in different shapes**. Both of them want the reader to get a self-consistent snapshot without blocking:

- A says: "the mutator builds the snapshot for you; just grab the pointer."
- B says: "the mutator changes things in place; you read, check the version, and confirm you got a coherent read."

A pushes the consistency work onto the mutator (build the new struct, swap one pointer). B pushes the consistency work onto the reader (read everything twice, check it matches).

A wins when mutations are rare and reads are frequent (which is exactly our case — boxes fire constantly, reconfigures land occasionally). B wins when you don't want to restructure the data layout. C is the brute-force option that works for any data layout but contradicts your stated preference.

## My take, expanded

For this codebase, I'd pick **A**, and here's the dataflow reason: **we already have one example of this pattern working well.** The connections array's "build a new array, copy + append, atomic-swap the pointer" dance IS option A, just specialised to one field. We'd be generalising it from "one field" to "all the per-port fields bundled together."

The cost is a one-time refactor: lift the per-port fields into a sub-struct and update the dispatch's reads to dereference through one extra pointer. That extra dereference happens once per fire and costs maybe 5 nanoseconds. The mutator gets slightly more work (allocate + populate a new struct instead of writing fields in place), but mutator work is rare.

Compared to B, A is **less code to write and harder to get subtly wrong**. The version-counter dance is one of those patterns where if you mis-order an operation by one line, you reintroduce the bug you were trying to fix, and the bug only shows up under timing pressure that's hard to reproduce. A's "build it complete, then publish" pattern is structurally hard to mess up — there's no in-between state that can be observed.

Compared to C, A is **kinder to the hot path**. Every box fire pays nothing for the consistency mechanism in option A (just one pointer load that would happen anyway). In option C, every box fire pays for a CAS that contended workers might lose.

Hopefully this is clearer? If you want me to draw out any of the three in even more detail, or work through a specific scenario (like "what happens when a reconfigure arrives while two readers are mid-fire"), just say which one.

## Assistant

Yes — your design is much cleaner. Let me answer your specific questions first, then walk through whether it works and where the rough edges are.

## How the ring buffers are associated with the box

Good news: **the ring buffers don't live inside the box at all.** They live in a separate place called the slot store, indexed by integer IDs. The box just holds an array of those IDs — one ID per input port — saying "input port 0 reads from slot 5, input port 1 reads from slot 7, input port 2 reads from slot 9."

The slot is a handle, like a row number in a database table. The box doesn't own the row; it just knows which row to look at.

```
box record:                  slot store (separate, shared):
  input_port_0 → slot id 5     slot 5: [ring buffer with values for port 0]
  input_port_1 → slot id 7     slot 7: [ring buffer with values for port 1]
  input_port_2 → slot id 9     slot 9: [ring buffer with values for port 2]
                               slot 12: [some other box's slot]
                               slot 13: [some other box's slot]
                               ...
```

**This is perfect for your design.** When you build the new box record, you copy the same slot IDs into it. Both boxes point at the same ring buffers — no copying, no moving, no "billion elements" problem. The ring buffers stay where they are; only the small metadata struct around them gets replaced.

Even better: pushers (other boxes that send values into these slots) look up the slot by ID too — they don't reach through the box record. So when boxes upstream are firing, they're pushing into the same slot whether they look up the OLD box or the NEW box. The dataflow into the slot is completely independent of the box-pointer dance.

## How task structs reference the box

Looking at the code: a task carries the box's **index number into the graph's box array**, not a pointer to the box record. So:

```
dispatch_task_t:
  box_id     = 7      ← the index, not a pointer
  task_id    = 42
  ctx        = pointer to the dispatch context (which holds the graph)
```

When the worker runs the task, it does `graph_box(graph, 7)` to resolve the index into the actual box record. That's the moment the worker grabs a pointer to the box. Until that moment, the task doesn't know anything about the box's memory address.

**This is also good for your design** — it means when a worker dequeues a task and resolves the index, it'll get whatever box is currently at that index. If you've already swapped to the new box, the worker gets the new one. If the swap is in progress (flag = 1), the worker sees the old box with flag = 1 and yields. The lookup is the natural place to inspect the flag.

There's a subtlety: the function pointer the spec invokes (the "lua_invoke" or "c_invoke" callback) is reached *through* the box (`spec_registry[box->spec_idx]->invoke`). So once the worker has the box pointer, it walks through the box to find the function. It does NOT cache the function pointer in the task struct. So if the new box has a different spec, the worker that follows the redirect will naturally call the new spec.

## Now for your design — does it work?

Yes, with one structural change and a few edge cases to think through.

**The structural change:** today the graph holds boxes as an array of records (`box_t boxes[N]`), so each box's memory address is stable for the whole run. To do your swap-the-whole-box dance, we need to change the graph to hold an array of **pointers** to boxes (`box_t *boxes[N]`). That way the mutator can build a new box record, swap the pointer in the array entry, and the old box record stays at its original address with whatever workers are still using it.

This is a one-time refactor — wherever `graph_box(g, i)` is called, the implementation switches from "return address of boxes[i]" to "return boxes[i]" — but the public API stays the same.

**The flag transitions:**
```
Flag state 0  ─►  normal. Workers proceed.
Flag state 1  ─►  reconfigure in progress. Workers yield-and-requeue.
Flag state 2  ─►  redirect. Workers follow box->next_box to the new box.
```

Worker arriving at a box:
1. Increment the box's **refcount** (atomic fetch-add, +1).
2. Check the flag.
3. If flag = 0: proceed normally, do the fire, decrement refcount at the end.
4. If flag = 1: decrement refcount, yield-and-requeue task to the back of the pool queue.
5. If flag = 2: decrement refcount on OLD box, follow `next_box` pointer to NEW box, restart from step 1 on the NEW box.

Mutator (Worker B) arriving with a reconfigure message:
1. Atomic CAS the flag from 0 → 1. If CAS fails (someone else is mutating, or someone already finished and we see flag = 2), yield-and-requeue our own task and exit.
2. Build the new box record with new fields. Copy slot IDs from old to new (same ring buffers).
3. Atomic store: `old_box->next_box = new_box_pointer`.
4. Atomic store: graph's pointer-array entry → new_box_pointer.
5. Atomic store: `old_box->flag = 2`. (Release-ordered, so anything we wrote before is visible.)
6. Walk away. We don't free the old box; whoever decrements refcount to zero will.

## Reference count — when modified, by whom

The refcount on a box is "**how many workers are currently using this exact box record.**" It's an atomic integer on the box.

- **Incremented** at the top of `dispatch_action`, right after the worker resolves the box pointer and BEFORE it checks the flag. The increment serves as a registration: "I'm here, don't free this box yet."
- **Decremented** at the bottom of `dispatch_action`, whether the fire succeeded, failed, or got redirected. The decrement signals "I'm done."

The worker that decrements the refcount to zero is responsible for the cleanup: if the decrement returned 0 AND the flag is 2 (meaning a redirect is in place, so no new workers will ever land here), that worker frees the old box record.

The "flag is 2" check matters because a freshly-created box with refcount briefly at 0 (between creations) shouldn't be freed — only a SUPERSEDED box should be. The flag = 2 says "superseded; if you're the last one out, turn off the lights."

## Will this system work — edge cases

Mostly yes. Here's my list of things to think through:

**1. The redirect-chain problem.** What if you reconfigure the box ten times in quick succession? Worker A is using v1. While A is still running, B reconfigures to v2 (chain: v1 → v2). Before A finishes, C reconfigures to v3 (chain: v1 → v2 → v3). A new worker arrives at v1, sees flag = 2, follows to v2, sees flag = 2, follows to v3. That works — but if you have a thousand reconfigures faster than A can finish, the chain could grow large and walking it gets expensive.

Mitigation: the mutator (B, then C) can update `v1->next_box` to point directly at the freshest box, not the immediate next. So C does `old_box = v2, new_box = v3` but also rewrites `v1->next_box = v3`. Now the chain is flat: anyone arriving at v1 jumps straight to v3. Each box's `next_box` always points at the freshest, not just the next.

**2. The graph pointer-array lookup race.** A new task gets dispatched on box index 7. By the time the worker resolves the index, the mutator has already swapped the pointer to the new box. The worker gets the new box, increments its refcount, runs normally. **This is fine** — the new box is the intended target. The old box was already superseded before the worker even looked.

The opposite case: a worker resolves the index, gets a pointer to the OLD box, and THEN the mutator swaps. The worker has the old pointer in a local variable, increments OLD's refcount, sees flag = 0 (because mutator hasn't flipped it yet), runs the fire on OLD. Mutator can't proceed past step 1 because the CAS would race — wait, no. The CAS on flag 0 → 1 succeeds regardless of refcount. The mutator flips OLD's flag to 1 while OLD's refcount is 1 (the worker). The mutator builds the new box. The worker, meanwhile, is mid-fire on OLD with the old config. The worker finishes, decrements refcount to 0. The mutator's flag transition 1 → 2 happens at some point. Free decision: the worker that decremented to 0 sees flag = 1 (not 2 yet) and DOESN'T free. The mutator finishes and sets flag = 2. Nobody is now decrementing, so the box leaks.

**Fix for that:** the free check should be "refcount == 0 AND flag = 2." But what if the mutator does its 1 → 2 transition and refcount is already 0? Nobody arrives to do the decrement-to-zero observation. So the mutator, after its 1 → 2 transition, should ALSO check refcount and free if 0. The rule becomes:
- Whoever does an action (decrement OR flag transition) that results in "refcount == 0 AND flag == 2" frees the box.

This is the classic "race to be the last actor" problem in reference counting. The pattern is well-understood: use a CAS to atomically claim "I am the freer," so only one party actually does the free.

**3. The slot store grows / shrinks.** If the reconfigure adds an input port that wasn't there before, a new slot needs to exist. The new box would hold a new slot ID. Allocating the slot can happen while the new box is being built (mutator does `slot_alloc` before swap). Removing a port is trickier — the old box still references the slot, and the old box is still being used by some workers. The slot can't be freed until the OLD box is freed. So slot lifetime is naturally tied to box refcount: when the old box gets freed, any slots it referenced that aren't in the new box get released.

But — wait. The slot might still have unconsumed values in its ring buffer! Should those be drained? Or dropped? That's a real design question for "remove a port mid-run." Probably worth a JSONL discard event for any leftover values.

**4. Does this remove the need for the connections array's append-only dance?** Yes. Once we have the swap-the-whole-box mechanism, runtime_connect (which today builds a new connections array and atomic-swaps the pointer) becomes redundant. It can use the same mechanism: build a new box with the new connection, swap. Simpler one path for ALL box mutations. **Strong argument for unifying.** The connections-only dance was a special case of the general problem; the general solution subsumes it.

**5. Reconfigure messages on the third ring.** The worker that pops a reconfigure cell from a slot's third ring is the one that becomes the mutator. It does the flag CAS, builds the new box, swaps. If the CAS fails (because someone else is already mutating), the worker yield-and-requeues with the message un-popped... actually no, the message IS popped. We'd need to either push it back (which violates the ring's normal semantics) or treat the mutator role as exclusive — if I can't grab the box, the reconfigure I just popped gets dropped and a JSONL event records it.

Hmm. Actually a cleaner shape: the worker that pops the reconfigure tag doesn't IMMEDIATELY apply it. It first CASes the flag. If CAS succeeds, THEN it pops the reconfigure and applies it. If CAS fails, leave the reconfigure on the ring and yield-and-requeue. The next worker that reaches the box and notices the reconfigure tag will retry.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The slot-as-handle decoupling is exactly what makes this design clean.** If the box owned its ring buffers directly (slot data inlined into the box struct), swapping the box would mean copying the rings — your "billion elements" worry. Because the box only holds integer IDs into the slot store, the box record is small and cheap to reallocate. The system's existing layered design earned this affordance — the people who built the slot store as a separate indexable thing in 302 didn't know they were enabling 320, but they were.
- **Refcounting + flag transitions is the classic "epoch-based reclamation" pattern.** Linux's RCU (Read-Copy-Update) uses this exact shape for kernel data structures that get rewritten while running: replace the pointer, wait for all readers to finish, then free. The only thing we're adding on top is the explicit flag state to gate new readers, which RCU usually handles by other means (grace periods). Your design is essentially "RCU with a per-object flag" — simpler to reason about, costs a CAS per fire instead of a grace-period mechanism. For occasional reconfigures and frequent reads, it's the right shape.
- **The "swap the whole box" pattern subsumes the connections-array pattern.** Once 320 lands, the special-case copy-and-publish on connections becomes a code smell — there's a more general mechanism that handles it. We'd retire the per-array dance in favor of "all mutations go through the box swap." That's a nice consolidation: one mutation discipline for the whole runtime instead of one-per-field.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

So my answers: yes this works, the structural prerequisite is converting the graph's box storage from "array of records" to "array of pointers," and the main edges are (1) the chain-flattening trick on `next_box`, (2) the "who frees" race using a CAS to claim freer status, (3) slot lifetime tied to old-box lifetime so removed ports don't get yanked out from under in-flight readers, and (4) the reconfigure-message handling needs to NOT pop the message until the flag CAS succeeds.

A question back to you: when a reconfigure changes the box's input port shape (removes a port that has values still queued in its slot), what should happen to those queued values? Drop them silently? Drop with a JSONL event? Drain them through a synthetic "discarded inputs" callback? This is the one bit I don't think the issue file has answered yet.

These refinements tighten the design significantly. Let me walk through each.

## On flag = 2 — yes, you can collapse it

You're right; the third state isn't needed. Here's the cleaner two-state version:

- **Flag 0:** nobody is mutating; readers proceed.
- **Flag 1:** a mutator owns this box; new arrivals yield-and-requeue.

What about the "find the new box" job that flag = 2 was doing? **The graph slot itself does that job.** Today the graph holds a pointer to each box at a numbered index. When the mutator atomically swaps that pointer from OLD-box-address to NEW-box-address, any worker that LATER looks up "box #7" gets the new box automatically. There's no need for OLD-box to know about NEW-box; OLD-box just sits there until its refcount expires.

So the dataflow becomes:

```
new worker arriving at box #7:
   1. look up graph slot #7 → get current box pointer (might be OLD or NEW
                                depending on swap timing — either is fine)
   2. increment refcount on whatever box you got
   3. peek the input rings on that box
   4. proceed (fire) or become-mutator or yield based on what you saw

mutator wrapping up:
   1. swap graph slot #7 → NEW
   2. (NEW box's flag is already 0; new arrivals find it cleanly)
   3. drop mutator's refcount on OLD
   4. OLD box drifts toward refcount=0 as its in-flight users finish
```

The "stale, re-push and re-queue" alternative you proposed for flag = 1 is also a valid shape, but it works at a finer granularity (mid-pop) than this design needs. Because we peek-everything-first before any pop (per your question 7), there's never a state where a worker has popped some values and then discovers the box is being mutated. The worker either knows about the reconfigure before any pops happen, or it commits to the whole fire under the current shape. No mid-fire bailout needed.

## On your refcount worry — you're catching a real ambiguity

You're right to push on this. I described refcount as "workers currently using" and that's incomplete. The complete picture has TWO sources of "still alive":

```
A box is alive when:
   (the graph slot still points at it)
   OR
   (refcount > 0 from in-flight workers + mutator)
```

A box is freeable only when BOTH:
```
   (graph slot points at something else, i.e. this box is superseded)
   AND
   (refcount has dropped to zero)
```

So for an idle box late in the pipeline that nobody is currently running: the graph slot points at it, refcount is 0, and that's fine — it stays alive on the "graph slot points at it" leg. It's not freeable because it hasn't been superseded.

Once a reconfigure happens and the slot swap occurs, the old box loses that "graph slot points at it" leg. Now refcount alone keeps it alive. If refcount was already 0 at the swap moment (no current workers), the box is immediately freeable — and the mutator (which is the one that did the swap) holds a +1 it took at the start, so the actual decrement-to-0 happens when the mutator drops its +1. That decrement is what triggers the free. **Your "mutator also holds a reference" intuition is exactly the bridge that makes this work.**

So the rule the freer follows:

```
when my refcount-decrement returns 0:
   if (graph slot points at me): do nothing. I'm still the live box.
   else: I've been superseded AND nobody is using me. Free me.
```

The mutator does that check when it drops its +1; if the old box had no other users, the mutator's decrement is the one that does the free.

## On the chain — agreed

Yeah, your reasoning is right. Worker A finishes in bounded time (one fire's worth), and any reconfigure chain past that is naturally cleaned up by the next refcount-to-zero. The chain can transiently grow but doesn't accumulate. As long as the garbage collection logic above works, the chain self-shrinks.

## On the orphaned-values question — confirmed, JSONL event

I'll spec the event shape:

```json
{"event":"reconfigure_discard", "box_id":"x",
 "port":"removed_port_name",
 "n_values_dropped":3,
 "first_value_bytes":"...truncated to 256...",
 "task_id":42}
```

One event per removed port (not one per value, to avoid log spam). First-value preview helps debugging without runaway log size.

## On unifying the connections dance with the box swap — issue file incoming

I'll write a new issue for "retire the connections-array atomic-publish pattern; route all box mutations through the box-swap mechanism from 320" and queue it for after 320 lands. I'll add it to the todo list now.

## On your pop-N-then-decide pattern — works, with one wrinkle

Your design is sound. The wrinkle is the "push values back into the ring buffers" step when the worker can't grab the CAS. Today's ring buffer doesn't have a "push to head" operation — it only pushes to the tail. So if Worker A pops value V from port 3's ring, then fails the CAS, then pushes V back, V goes to the BACK of the queue, behind any new values that arrived in the meantime. That breaks the arrival-order property.

There are three workable shapes here:

**Shape 1 — Add a push-to-head operation to the ring.** Symmetric with pop-from-head. Used only on the recovery path. Implementation cost is small; the ring already has a head pointer.

**Shape 2 — Peek instead of pop.** The worker peeks each port's next tag (looks at it without consuming). If no reconfigure, proceeds to actually pop. If any reconfigure, tries CAS. On CAS success, pops everything. On CAS fail, hasn't committed anything; just yields. Cleaner because there's nothing to put back.

**Shape 3 — Hold values in worker-local memory until the decision is made.** The pop happens, but the values live in the worker's stack until either the fire commits or the worker becomes the mutator and decides what to do with them. On CAS-fail, the worker re-pushes (tail), accepting the reorder for that specific case.

I'd vote **Shape 2** because it doesn't require any new ring operations and doesn't have a reorder edge case at all. The worker's commitment point is "I started popping" — and it only starts popping after it knows no reconfigure is pending. The peek-all-then-pop-all dance reads cleanly: "first look at every door without opening it, then once you're sure nothing weird is behind any of them, open them all."

For a dual-ring slot, we'd add a peek-ordered operation alongside the existing pop-ordered, returning just the tag from the next ordering ring entry without removing it. That's a small addition.

Either way, the journey you described ends the same way: the worker has the values in the right order, the task struct gets populated, the spec's invoke runs. 

## Questions back to you

1. **Push-to-head vs. peek-first** — Shape 1, 2, or 3 above?

2. **What does the reconfigure tag carry in the case of an EMPTY box** (no input ports)? Without input ports there's no third ring to put the message on. Does a box with no inputs simply lose the ability to be reconfigured by message? Or do we add a "control ring" attached to the box itself, separate from input ports? My instinct: the control ring is cleaner and generalizes (control messages aren't tied to a port, they're tied to the box). It also feels right because a reconfigure message isn't conceptually an "input value" — it's a control plane thing.

3. **Reconfiguring a box that's in the middle of being reconfigured** — Worker B is mutating. Worker C arrives with a second reconfigure message in hand. C tries the CAS, fails (flag = 1). What should C do? Three options:
   - **a)** C drops its reconfigure message to JSONL ("couldn't apply, mutator busy"). 
   - **b)** C pushes its reconfigure back to the third ring (where the second-worker-to-try-CAS will retry). Risk: infinite loop if mutators keep contending.
   - **c)** C waits with peek-style semantics — leaves the message on the ring, requeues itself, retries naturally on next dispatch. Same as the general yield-and-requeue path.
   - I'd vote (c) — same mechanism as everything else, no special case.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The graph slot acting as a refcount holder is the conceptually beautiful part of your design.** It naturally encodes "the runtime considers this box current" without needing a separate "is_live" flag. When a box gets superseded, its lifetime collapses from "until the run ends" to "until in-flight users finish" — that transition is captured entirely by the graph slot losing its pointer, no extra bookkeeping. The same pattern shows up in epoch-based reclamation and hazard pointers; you've reinvented a chunk of well-tested concurrent-GC theory by reasoning from first principles, which is the highest compliment a design pattern can pay you.
- **Your pop-N-then-decide instinct catches a structural truth about reconfigure messages.** They aren't normal inputs — they're control plane masquerading as data. The reason they ride the same per-port slot system is convenience (the producer already knows how to reach a port), not because they ARE input values. So the "decide whether to fire OR mutate based on what's queued" pattern reflects the actual semantics: control-plane traffic preempts data-plane traffic. The peek-all-first design makes this preemption clean.
- **The two-state flag + graph slot-as-liveness is structurally simpler than three states.** Every state machine should have as few states as it can get away with — fewer states = fewer transitions = fewer race conditions. Going from three states to two doesn't sound like much, but the THIRD state was the source of most of the "what does free decision look like" complexity in my version. Killing it killed half the edge cases.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Assistant

You're catching a really deep problem and proposing a really clever solution. Let me work through both halves separately.

## On garbage collection paranoia

You've hit on something fundamental: **in a system that allows runtime creation and reconfiguration, you genuinely cannot prove a box will never run again.** Any currently-running box could call create_box-with-connection-to-Y, or reconfigure-some-existing-box-to-connect-to-Y. There's no finite analysis that says "this box is unreachable forever."

This is the same problem Java's GC solves with mark-and-sweep, and it's the same problem the Linux kernel deals with via epoch-based reclamation. The general lesson: **you can't reason locally about reachability in a system that allows arbitrary mutation.** You either need a global walk (expensive) or you accept the leak.

For this codebase, I'd actually argue the right answer is: **don't try to GC live boxes at all.** Here's the reasoning chain:

- The refcount system handles SUPERSEDED boxes (the reconfigure cleanup case). That's the only "lifetime ends mid-run" we genuinely need.
- For boxes that just sit idle with no incoming values — they're not freed, they're just dormant. They consume metadata-sized memory (one box record + slot ids), which is tiny. The values that would otherwise pile up in their slot ring buffers aren't there, because no producer is feeding them.
- For the case where a user genuinely wants a box destroyed during a run: add an explicit `runtime_destroy_box(id)` API. The user knows when their planner box is done planning. Trust them.
- Runs are bounded. When the run ends (quiescence), everything gets cleaned up by graph_destroy.

The leak you're worried about — "what if we have too many boxes cluttering up memory" — would only show up in extremely long-running maps that constantly create new boxes without destroying old ones. Each box is ~200 bytes plus its slots. You'd need to create literally millions of boxes for this to matter on a workstation. Maps that long would have other architectural concerns anyway.

**My proposal:** refcount handles superseded-box reclamation (automatic, transparent). Explicit destroy handles user-requested-destruction (rare, opt-in). Nothing tries to be clever about reachability. The leak case is real but practically negligible.

## On the multi-reconfigure question

OK so let me try to simplify what you described, because I think there's a cleaner shape hiding inside your description, and the cleaner shape eliminates the "what if values for first but not second" edge case entirely.

You're absolutely right that **peeking the ordering messages first (without popping any values) is the right move.** That part is the key insight. Let me re-state it in your terms: every input ring has, riding alongside the native and json rings, a third ring that's the **ordering ring** — it stores tags saying "the next value on this slot is in the native ring" or "...is in the json ring" or "this is a reconfigure message, not a value." The ordering ring's head tells you what kind of thing is up next, without committing to consume the actual value or message yet.

So the worker's hot path becomes:

```
worker arrives at box:
   1. for each input port, peek the head of its ordering ring (just the tag, no consume).
   2. branch on what you saw:
        no reconfigures anywhere AND all ports have a value waiting:
           → pop one ordering tag from each port,
             follow each tag to its native/json ring,
             pop the value,
             fire the box.

        at least one port shows RECONFIGURE at its head:
           → try CAS to become the mutator. yield if you lose.

        no reconfigures, but some ports are empty:
           → don't pop anything. nothing to fire yet. exit; the pool
             will re-spawn this box when more values arrive.
```

Now for the multi-reconfigure case — here's where I want to push back on the "build multiple tasks for multiple epochs" idea. **I don't think we need it.** Let me show why with your scenario.

### Walking through your hard case

Setup: port X has `[R1, v1]`. Port Y has `[R2, v3]`. Two reconfigures (one per port), one fire's worth of values.

Worker arrives. Peeks X → R1. Peeks Y → R2. Both reconfigures visible. Worker becomes mutator. What now?

The thing to notice: **R1 and R2 are on DIFFERENT ports.** They're not temporally ordered with respect to each other. The producer that pushed R1 onto X did so at some wall-clock moment; the producer that pushed R2 onto Y did so at some other wall-clock moment; the ring buffer system doesn't preserve cross-port ordering. So there's no "right" order for R1 vs R2 — apply them in any order, doesn't matter.

Mutator pops R1, applies it. Now the box has shape-after-R1. Mutator pops R2, applies it. Now the box has shape-after-R1-and-R2. Mutator releases.

Next worker arrives. Peeks X → v1 (head of X now). Peeks Y → v3 (head of Y now). Both are values. Pops both, fires the box with v1 and v3 under the shape-after-R1-and-R2.

**There's only one fire, and it happens after both reconfigures are applied.** The "different epochs" framing doesn't apply because the values v1 and v3 were queued BEHIND the reconfigure messages on their respective ports. So they were always meant for the post-reconfigure shape — that's what the producer signaled by ordering them that way on the ring.

If the producer had wanted v1 consumed under the pre-reconfigure shape, they would have pushed v1 FIRST, then R1. The ring would be `[v1, R1]`. Peek would show v1; a fire would happen with v1 under OLD shape; then peek would show R1; reconfigure applies; the next fire would be under NEW.

### Now the harder case — back-to-back reconfigures

Setup: port X has `[R1, R2, v1]`. Port Y has `[v3]`.

Worker peeks X → R1. Peeks Y → v3. Has a reconfigure pending. Becomes mutator.

The mutator's loop:
- Pop R1 from X. Apply it.
- Peek X again. Head is now R2 (because R1 got popped). 
- Pop R2. Apply it.
- Peek X again. Head is now v1. Not a reconfigure.
- Release mutator role. Don't fire here; let a worker do that.

Next worker arrives. Peeks X → v1, peeks Y → v3. Both values. Pop, fire.

**One fire, after both R1 and R2 are applied.** No multi-task batching.

### And the case you specifically asked about

Setup: port X has `[R1, R2]`. Port Y has `[v3]`. Two reconfigures, only one port has a value.

Worker peeks X → R1. Becomes mutator. Pops R1, applies. Peeks X → R2. Pops R2, applies. Peeks X → empty. Releases.

Next worker arrives. Peeks X → empty. Peeks Y → v3. Mixed — some ports have values, some don't. Worker can't fire. Doesn't pop anything. Exits.

The next time someone pushes a value to X, the pool will spawn a task for the box. Next worker arrives, peeks X → (whatever's there now). If it's a value AND Y still has v3, fires. Otherwise waits.

**No edge case to handle.** The "we have enough values for the first reconfigure but not the second" scenario doesn't actually exist, because reconfigures are NOT consumers of values. They're sideband messages that modify the box. Values are consumed by FIRES, and fires only happen when every required port has a value ready.

### Why I think the multi-task batching isn't needed

Your design described the mutator preparing multiple tasks for different box epochs. But under the simpler "do one thing per worker visit" model:

- Reconfigure messages get drained (in batches if multiple are visible at peek time, but only one batch per mutator visit).
- Fires happen separately, each one under the box's current shape at fire time.
- A worker either fires once OR mutates once per visit; never both, never multiple of either.

The pool's natural re-spawning handles the back-and-forth. If there are 5 reconfigures and 5 fires worth of values queued, the workflow is:
- Worker 1 visits: drains all visible reconfigures, releases.
- Worker 2 visits: drains any reconfigures that became visible after the first batch (because they were behind the first batch on the rings), or fires if values are next up.
- ...
- Eventually all reconfigures are drained and fires proceed normally.

This is slightly more pool-queue traffic than the "do everything in one mutator visit" design, but each pool round-trip is cheap (microseconds). The complexity savings are large — no multi-task batching, no epoch tracking, no "store this task pointing at the OLD box, submit it, store another pointing at NEW, submit that."

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The "you can't prove a box won't run again" realisation is the same as the halting problem in disguise.** In a Turing-complete dataflow system (which is what we are, since boxes can create boxes and connect them dynamically), you can't statically determine reachability. The Java GC people solved this with mark-sweep; Linux kernel people solved it with epoch-based reclamation; everybody else solves it with explicit destroy. Your project's vision is closer to "explicit destroy" because runs are bounded — when the run ends, everything frees. The leak you were worried about doesn't actually accumulate across runs, just within a single run, which is bounded.
- **The "epoch tasks" idea is reinventing event sourcing.** Your instinct was: instead of mutating state and losing the history, capture the history (each reconfigure is an event) and replay tasks against each state in the history. That's exactly what event sourcing does in distributed systems. It's powerful but adds significant complexity. For this codebase, the "ring buffer ordering IS the history" framing is sufficient — values queued behind a reconfigure are implicitly "for the post-reconfigure shape." The implicit ordering replaces explicit epoch tracking.
- **The peek-don't-pop discipline solves the push-back problem entirely.** You arrived at this independently and correctly: if you commit nothing until you've inspected everything, there's nothing to put back if you bail. The same pattern shows up in database transactions (read-then-decide-then-commit), in optimistic concurrency control, and in modern parser combinators (peek-don't-consume until you know the production matches). It's a strong general pattern: in any concurrent system, prefer "look first, commit later" over "commit first, undo if wrong."
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Questions back

1. **Do you buy the "no multi-task batching, do one thing per worker visit" simplification?** It dramatically simplifies the mutator's logic and eliminates the edge case you were worried about. The cost is more pool round-trips when many reconfigures are queued — but that cost is small.

2. **Are you OK with "no GC of live boxes, only superseded ones"?** Plus an explicit `runtime_destroy_box(id)` API for users who want to actively clean up? That sidesteps the unreachability halting problem cleanly.

3. **One subtle thing about the mutator's "drain multiple visible reconfigures in one go" loop** — should there be a cap on how many it'll drain before releasing? If a malicious or buggy producer keeps pushing reconfigures faster than the mutator can apply them, the mutator could be stuck draining forever. A cap (say, 100 reconfigures per mutator visit, then release and let someone else continue) would be a safety valve. Worth adding, or YAGNI?

Once you've signed off on these, I'll update 320's issue file with the consolidated design and start implementing.

## Assistant

You found a genuine race, and it's the deepest problem we've hit yet. Let me think through it carefully because the answer reshapes some of what we've decided.

## Walking through what you just identified

Let me restate to make sure I understand: two workers, A and B, both arrive at the same box at roughly the same time. Both peek every input port's head. Both see `[v1, v2, v3]`. Both decide "no reconfigures, proceed to pop." A wins the pop race and gets `[v1, v2, v3]`. B's pops return `[v4, v5, R1]` — because v1, v2, v3 are gone, what B actually gets is whatever is NOW at the head of each ring.

B has popped a reconfigure message it never saw during peek. B's choice is now: apply the reconfigure (but it already popped v4 and v5, which were meant for who?), or somehow undo. Both are bad.

**Your three suggestions, evaluated:**

- *Should peeking be atomic?* Atomic across what? Atomic peek of a single ring is already true. Atomic peek across THREE rings simultaneously is the hard part — it would essentially require locking all three rings at once, which is the very serialization we wanted to avoid.

- *Should peek return a pointer and advance the index?* This is "soft pop" — reserve but don't release. The problem: if B bails (because it sees a reconfigure in its reserved set), the reservation has to be released. And once released, A might already have pop-committed past the reserved spot. We're in the same push-back problem we tried to avoid, just dressed up in different clothes.

The honest answer: **peek-then-pop cannot be made race-free across multiple rings without serializing the workers somewhere.** The race is inherent to "two workers reading different rings at the same time."

## When this race can actually happen

Here's a saving grace: **the race only exists for boxes that allow multiple concurrent fires.** Most boxes in this system are single-spawn — only one task at a time gathers for them. For those, A and B can't both be gathering simultaneously, so the race doesn't appear.

The multi-spawn case (iterators, certain runtime-created boxes) is where this bites. The graph loader marks each box with whether it's multi-spawn-capable (the `multi_spawn` field on the box record).

So the cost of solving this only has to be paid by multi-spawn boxes. Single-spawn boxes get the simpler peek-then-pop dance for free.

## Proposal — gather serialization on multi-spawn boxes only

The cleanest answer I can see: **on multi-spawn boxes, the gather phase is serialized through a per-box gather lock.** It's a CAS, brief (microseconds), and only contended when multiple workers genuinely want to fire the same box at the same time.

This means we'd revise your earlier principle slightly. The original framing was "workers don't take YARQ — they're just reading values, not mutating them." But popping values from a shared ring IS a mutation; it changes the slot's head pointer and removes the value from the ring. When two workers pop from the same set of rings concurrently, they're racing on shared mutable state.

So a more precise version of your principle: **workers don't take YARQ for reading box METADATA (the input port list, the function pointer, the connections array). But for popping values from rings shared across multi-spawn fires, workers DO need brief serialization.**

This separates two different concerns:
- **YARQ-for-mutation** (rare, taken by reconfigure / runtime_connect / box-swap operations).
- **Gather-lock** (frequent on multi-spawn boxes, brief, just guards the peek-and-pop atomicity).

They could be the same atomic word with different semantics, or two separate words. I'd lean toward separate words: the gather lock is needed thousands of times per run; the YARQ is needed rarely. Conflating them means the rare reconfigure has to wait for every concurrent gather to finish, which is fine but loses some opportunity.

Or — alternative — single-spawn boxes simply don't need a gather lock at all (because there's only one gatherer ever), so they skip it. Multi-spawn boxes take the gather lock for the duration of the peek-pop window.

## On your other points

**Read boxes have no inputs, so no ring for reconfigure messages.** Agreed — delete-and-recreate is the right answer. Read boxes are stateless cached values; rebuilding one is cheap.

**"create-a-box" takes the same struct as reconfigure; same-ID means reconfigure.** YES, this is a great unification. The runtime mutation API collapses to one entry point: `runtime_apply_box_spec(spec_json)`. The dispatch reads the `id` field:
- If the id is new → create.
- If the id exists → reconfigure.

This is structurally honest: creating a box and reconfiguring a box are both "express what this box should be." The implementation difference (allocate-then-publish vs swap-and-supersede) is a runtime detail, not a user-facing distinction.

**"check CAS before popping"** — yes, exactly right. This is the same point as my proposed peek-first design, just stated more clearly: workers commit to nothing until they know the box is safe to operate on. If a worker sees a reconfigure message while peeking, it CASes first; if CAS fails, no values were touched, no rollback needed.

**Box delete = "create" with just ID, nothing else.** Lovely. The minimum to supply is the ID and a flag like `"delete": true` (or some marker that distinguishes "delete me" from "I forgot to fill in other fields"). I'd suggest an explicit `"action": "delete"` field to avoid ambiguity. Deleted boxes get their refcount-driven cleanup via the same mechanism as superseded boxes. I'll write an issue file for this.

## What I want to confirm with you before writing the design up

1. **Are you OK with workers taking a brief gather lock on multi-spawn boxes?** This is the cleanest answer to the race you found. It's a CAS, uncontended in the common case (most boxes are single-spawn), brief in the contended case (microseconds). Your earlier principle was "workers don't take YARQ" — I'm proposing a refinement where "workers don't take YARQ for METADATA reads, but DO take a brief gather-lock for popping values on multi-spawn boxes."

2. **Separate locks or the same lock?** Option (a): one atomic word per box, used both for mutation and for multi-spawn gather. Simpler. Cost: rare reconfigures wait for any current gather to finish, which is fine. Option (b): two atomic words, one for each purpose. Slightly faster but more bookkeeping. I'd vote (a) for simplicity.

3. **The unified `runtime_apply_box_spec` — should it also handle the delete case?** I.e., one entry point that does create / reconfigure / delete based on the spec's content?

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The race you found is a textbook example of "linearizability across multiple objects."** Each ring buffer is independently atomic, but the CROSS-RING operation (peek every port, then pop every port) is not. This is the same problem distributed databases solve with two-phase commit: you can either serialize the cross-object operation, or you can accept that some operations might observe a transient inconsistent state and need to retry. Your project is small enough that serialization (per-box gather lock) is cheap. For larger systems, the trade-off shifts toward lock-free designs with retry loops.
- **The "single-spawn vs multi-spawn" distinction is doing a LOT of work in this design.** Iterator boxes are basically the only multi-spawn case in normal use. By scoping the gather lock to multi-spawn boxes, we pay the synchronization cost exactly where it's actually needed — and the vast majority of fires (call boxes with simple inputs) cost nothing extra. The graph loader already computes this attribute, so we get the scoping for free.
- **"Workers don't claim YARQ" was the right principle, with an important refinement: it applies to BOX-METADATA reads, not to RING POPS.** Reading the input port list is non-destructive; that's where the original principle applies — readers shouldn't pay for the writers' work. Popping a value is destructive; that genuinely needs some coordination among concurrent poppers. The refined principle preserves the original intent (no YARQ for metadata) while honestly acknowledging that popping is a different beast.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

If you sign off on the gather-lock proposal, I'll consolidate everything we've discussed — two-state flag, graph-slot-as-liveness, refcount + mutator-holds-reference, peek-don't-pop, per-multi-spawn-box gather lock, unified runtime_apply_box_spec — into 320's issue file. Then we can start implementing.

## Assistant

That correction matters — thank you. And your proposal is genuinely the cleanest version I've heard. Let me confirm it works, address the storage question (which is the one real open detail), and then we can move to implementation.

## Your design, restated

Every box has a single atomic flag. State 0 means "free." State 1 means "someone is in the gather phase right now." When a worker arrives at the box, it tries to CAS the flag from 0 to 1. If it wins, it enters the gather section. If it loses, it yields-and-requeues — the pool will hand it back later.

Inside the gather section, the worker is the only thread touching this box's input rings. It can safely peek the heads of all the ordering rings without worrying about another worker racing on the same values. Then it makes a single decision:

- **All heads are values (native or json):** pop them all, release the flag, hand the values back to the worker to fire the spec.
- **Any head is a reconfigure:** pop the reconfigure message, apply it (build new box, swap the graph slot), release the flag. Don't fire. The worker either submits a new task for the (now-new) box or just returns — the pool will naturally spawn fresh tasks when producers push more values.

A worker visit is **exactly one of**: a fire or a mutation. Never both. Never partial.

This eliminates every race we've been discussing. The gather lock holds for microseconds. Workers on DIFFERENT boxes don't contend at all — the flag is per-box. Workers on the SAME box serialize through gather, which is the only way to make peek-then-pop honest in a multi-threaded setting.

## On your storage question

You're asking exactly the right question. The input ring buffers hold values, and a typical value is something like an integer or a short string. Reconfigure messages are variable-length JSON — could be a few hundred bytes, could be many kilobytes. They don't fit the same shape.

There are two ways to attach reconfigure messages to a box. I want to lay them both out and ask you which you prefer, because the choice ripples through the rest of the design:

**Way 1 — A third ring on every input slot, with a "this cell is a reconfigure" tag.**
- Producers push reconfigure messages onto whichever port they reach.
- The slot already has native + json data rings (from the dual-ring work in 312). We add a third "reconfigure" data ring per slot, storing the JSON payload via the large-value heap (so variable-size is handled cleanly).
- The ordering ring's tags grow a third possible value — RECONFIGURE — telling the consumer "the head of the ordering ring points at the reconfigure ring, not native or json."
- Gather peeks every port's ordering ring as part of its scan.
- Empty boxes (no input ports) can't receive reconfigure messages this way — they need delete-and-recreate (which you already agreed to).

**Way 2 — A single per-box control queue, completely separate from any input port.**
- Each box has its own small ring buffer attached directly to it (not to any port), specifically for control messages like reconfigure.
- The runtime API `runtime_apply_box_spec(target_id, spec_json)` pushes onto the target box's control queue. The producer doesn't need to know about ports — it just names the box.
- Gather checks the control queue first. If non-empty, become mutator. Otherwise, peek the input ports and decide normally.
- Works for boxes with zero input ports (they have a control queue too).
- The reconfigure JSON lives in the large-value heap, with the control queue cell holding a handle to it.

**My read:** Way 2 is simpler and more honest about what reconfigure messages are. They're not input values riding on a port; they're control plane messages targeted at a specific box. Treating them as a separate channel is cleaner than overloading the port mechanism.

The reason I originally went with Way 1 in the issue file was that the user (you) described it that way, and producers-already-know-how-to-reach-a-port was the rationale. But now that we have the `runtime_apply_box_spec` unification (the runtime API that handles create / reconfigure / delete based on the spec's content), the producer doesn't need to push through a port at all. The runtime API can find the target box directly.

I'd vote Way 2.

## Walking through your full design end-to-end with Way 2

Setup: box B has three input ports (X, Y, Z), all multi-spawn. Box B has a control queue (initially empty). Box B's gather flag is 0.

**Scenario 1 — normal fire:**

Producer A pushes a value onto port X. Producer B pushes onto port Y. Producer C pushes onto port Z. Three workers (W1, W2, W3) are spawned for box B (because all input ports are ready).

W1 arrives first. CAS flag 0 → 1. Wins. Enters gather. Checks control queue: empty. Peeks port X, Y, Z: all values. Pops from each ordering ring; each tag tells W1 which data ring to pop from (native or json). W1 has three values in hand. Releases flag (set to 0). Calls the spec's invoke with the three values.

W2 arrives. CAS flag 0 → 1. Might win or lose depending on timing.
- If wins: enters gather, finds rings empty (W1 just drained them), can't fire. Releases. The pool will respawn W2 if more values arrive.
- If loses: yields. Pool requeues it.

W3 same story.

**Scenario 2 — reconfigure arrives:**

Producer pushes a value onto port X. Then someone calls `runtime_apply_box_spec(B, new_spec_json)`. The runtime API pushes the spec onto box B's control queue.

Worker W1 arrives. CAS flag 0 → 1. Wins. Enters gather. Checks control queue: non-empty. **Decision: mutate, not fire.** Pops the spec from the control queue. Builds a new box record (call it B'). Atomically swaps the graph slot's pointer from B to B'. Drops the mutator's refcount on B. Releases the flag (sets B's flag back to 0 — though it doesn't really matter, B is now superseded).

What about the value that producer pushed onto port X? It's still in the ring buffer attached to slot ID for port X. **And here's the key — the new box B' carries the SAME slot IDs.** The slots themselves didn't move; they belong to the slot store, identified by integer IDs. The value is still queued, ready for the next worker to consume under B's new shape.

The next worker that arrives at box B follows the graph slot, gets B' (the new box), CASes flag 0 → 1, enters gather, peeks port X, sees the value waiting, pops it, fires under B's new shape.

**Scenario 3 — reconfigure during contention:**

Worker W1 holds the gather flag (state 1) on box B, doing a normal fire's worth of work. Meanwhile, someone calls `runtime_apply_box_spec(B, ...)` to push a reconfigure into B's control queue. Worker W2 arrives at B. CAS flag 0 → 1 fails (W1 holds it). W2 yields-and-requeues.

W1 finishes its gather, releases the flag (sets to 0). W2 (or another worker) eventually arrives. CAS wins. Enters gather. Checks control queue: non-empty. Becomes mutator. Applies reconfigure. Done.

Notice the ordering: W1's fire happens BEFORE the reconfigure is applied. The reconfigure arrived chronologically during W1's gather, but the gather lock serialized the operations cleanly — W1 finishes, then W2 mutates. The reconfigure lands on the "next opportunity" rather than racing in.

## Resolving the YARQ principle

Your original phrasing was "workers who are just executing the box function should not claim the yarq. they don't need it - they're just reading values, not mutating them." The refined version that this design exemplifies:

- **The box function itself runs WITHOUT holding any lock.** Once the values are gathered and the worker has them in hand, the spec's invoke runs in parallel with anything else on any other thread. That's where the "no YARQ for execution" principle holds tight.
- **The gather phase IS a brief atomic section.** It's per-box, microseconds long, and unavoidable in a multi-threaded design where multiple workers might be pulling from the same rings.

In other words, the principle was right for the FIRE phase (where the spec's invoke runs and can be fully parallel), and the gather phase is just a small serialization tax that buys us correctness. Most code execution time is in fire, so the parallel-execution property is preserved where it matters.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **"A worker visit is either a fire OR a mutation, never both" is the principle that makes everything else work.** Without it, you have to reason about partial states ("I started gathering but then realised I should mutate; what about the values I already popped?"). With it, the gather section has exactly two exit paths, and each one is internally consistent. This is the dataflow equivalent of a database transaction's all-or-nothing property — either the whole gather happens or none of it does, and the choice is decided at one well-defined moment.
- **The per-box gather lock + per-box graph slot pointer is a complete concurrent-object lifecycle in two atomic words.** One word governs "who's allowed to mutate me right now"; the other governs "am I still the live version of this id." Everything else — refcounts, freeing, supersession — follows from those two. It's a surprisingly compact representation of a famously hard problem.
- **Way 2 (per-box control queue) reflects a structural truth: control plane and data plane are different things and shouldn't share the same channel.** In networking, this is why DNS, ARP, ICMP exist separately from TCP/UDP data flow — control messages have different ordering requirements, different priorities, different consumer logic. Your dataflow runtime has the same shape; reconfigure messages aren't input values, they're sideband signals that modify the data plane's behaviour. Giving them their own channel makes the system honest about its layering.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## What I want to confirm before writing the design up

1. **Way 1 (third ring per port) vs Way 2 (per-box control queue) for reconfigure message storage?** I'd vote Way 2; lighter, generalises to empty boxes, more honest about control-vs-data.

2. **One atomic word per box governing both gather AND mutation?** State 0 = free; state 1 = someone is in the gather section (which might be a fire OR a mutation, decided once we look at the control queue). This collapses the "YARQ for mutation, separate gather lock for popping" idea into one word. Simpler. The cost: a rare mutator waits for at most one in-flight gather to finish, which is microseconds.

3. **Are we done with the design phase?** If you sign off on these two, I'll consolidate everything we've discussed into the 320 issue file, add the connections-unification follow-up issue, and the box-delete issue. Then we can move to implementation.

## On the YARQ-after-reconfigure correction

You're correct, and my earlier description had a bug. Here's the scenario:

Producer pushes a value onto port X of box B. The pool spawns a task for box B (because B has inputs ready). Meanwhile, someone pushes a reconfigure onto B's control queue. Worker W1 arrives, wins the gather flag, checks the control queue, decides to mutate, applies the reconfigure, swaps the graph slot, releases the flag, and returns.

But the value on port X is still sitting in its ring. Nobody scheduled a fresh task — because no producer pushed anything new (producers don't push for nothing; the push that originally caused the spawn was already consumed by the spawn decision).

The value just sits there, orphaned. Eventually the run quiesces with this dangling value, and the box never fires.

The fix: **the mutator yields-and-requeues itself instead of just returning.** It tells the pool "I'm not actually done; please put me back at the tail of the queue." The pool re-queues a task for box B. Eventually some worker picks it up, arrives at B (which now points at the NEW box struct via the graph slot), wins the gather flag, peeks the inputs, sees the value waiting, and fires under the new shape.

This is the same yield-and-requeue mechanism we already use when a worker can't grab the gather flag (because someone else has it). One unified path: any time a worker arrives at a box and doesn't actually fire, it yarqs.

## On the "point the box at the reconfigure input" idea

This is wonderful. Let me make sure I'm reading you right: the reconfigure message doesn't need to be JSON that the mutator parses — it can already BE a fully-built box struct, sitting in memory, ready to use. The mutator's job is just to take that pointer and atomically substitute it into the graph slot.

If that's what you mean, then yes, this is the right design. Walking through the consequences:

**Who builds the new box struct?**

Whoever calls the runtime API. For an internal C caller, that's straightforward — they construct the struct directly. For a Lua box calling `soramech.reconfigure(target_id, {kind="call", fn="new_fn", ...})`, the Lua language bridge code does the build: it reads the Lua table, allocates a new box struct, fills in the fields, and hands the pointer to the runtime. The bridge does the same kind of work it already does today for `create_box` — it's the same shape.

For a "reconfigure box" kind (where some box receives a JSON spec on an input port and pushes a reconfigure), that box's spec invoke parses the JSON and builds the struct.

In all cases: by the time the message reaches the target box's control queue, it's already a pre-built struct pointer. No parsing happens inside the gather lock.

**What does the control queue actually hold?**

Just pointers. Each cell in the control queue is one pointer (8 bytes). A small ring — say, 8 cells per box — is enough to absorb bursts of reconfigures. If the ring fills up (which would require 8 reconfigures arriving in microseconds, faster than any worker can drain them), pushers block briefly waiting for space.

No JSON bytes ever live in a ring. No large-value heap involvement for control messages. The cells are tiny fixed-size pointer slots.

**What does the mutator actually do?**

```
mutator wins the gather flag:
   check control queue. it's non-empty.
   pop one box-struct pointer (call it NEW).
   resolve port mappings: for each port in NEW, find the matching
       slot id from OLD (rename by name match), or allocate a new
       slot if the port is brand new.
   patch NEW's input_slot_ids[] with the resolved IDs.
   atomically swap the graph slot pointer from OLD to NEW.
   release the gather flag.
   yarq.
```

The slot-resolution step is the only non-trivial work. Ports that exist in both OLD and NEW (matched by name) share the same slot ID — the underlying ring buffer keeps all its queued values intact across the reconfigure. Ports that are new in NEW get freshly allocated slots. Ports that existed in OLD but not in NEW are abandoned — their slot IDs aren't in NEW, so nobody reads from them anymore; their remaining values get logged to the JSONL discard event you specified, and the slot itself can be released when OLD is freed.

**What about the OLD box struct?**

It stays alive as long as any in-flight worker is using it (refcount > 0). The graph slot now points at NEW, so no new workers will land on OLD. When OLD's refcount hits zero, it's freed.

The free includes freeing the box struct's malloc'd memory (the struct itself) and releasing any per-box state that the new box doesn't share (like the slots for ports that were removed).

## What this gives us

The reconfigure mechanism becomes structurally tiny:

- The control queue is a fixed-size ring of pointers. Cheap.
- The runtime API takes a box struct pointer. Caller builds it; runtime takes ownership.
- The mutator's work is: resolve slots, swap graph pointer, release, yarq. No JSON parsing on the hot path. No variable-size storage. No "where does the message live" question because the message IS the new box.

It also means the **"create" and "reconfigure" operations converge even more cleanly** than we discussed before. Both are just "here's a new box struct, please install it." Create: install at a fresh graph slot. Reconfigure: install at an existing graph slot, supersede the old occupant. Delete: install NULL at the graph slot, supersede the old occupant.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **"The message IS the new box" is the same pattern as immutable persistent data structures.** In Clojure or Haskell, you don't mutate a data structure in place — you produce a new version and let the runtime swap pointers. Old versions stick around as long as anyone references them. Your reconfigure design lands in the same place by reasoning about concurrent dataflow: the mutator builds a new immutable thing and atomically swaps in the new reference. Everyone holding the old reference finishes their work cleanly; the old thing eventually goes away. The pattern shows up independently in functional programming, in Linux RCU, and now in your dataflow runtime — three different domains converging on the same answer because the problem shape is the same.
- **Putting the build work on the producer's side is a load-balancing move.** Parsing JSON, validating fields, allocating arrays — all of that happens in whoever called the runtime API, in their own time, on their own thread. The mutator's critical section (the gather lock hold) shrinks to "swap pointer, resolve a few slot IDs." That's microseconds. The slower work (the parse) doesn't block anyone else's work on the same box.
- **The slot-id stability across reconfigures is the unsung hero of the whole design.** Because port names map to stable slot IDs, the ring buffers don't need to move. A port "x" with 100 queued values stays exactly where it is across a reconfigure. The new box just keeps referencing the same slot ID. That's the cheapest possible transition: zero bytes copied.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`
