## Assistant

All three points push the design in good directions. Let me respond.

## On using a ring buffer

You're completely right — I was overthinking the naming. The control queue IS a ring buffer; that's exactly the pattern. And we should use the same primitive we use everywhere else: the slot store. A box's control queue is just another slot allocated from the slot store, sized to hold pointer-cells.

Concretely: at box creation time, the runtime allocates one extra slot per box flagged as the control slot. Cell capacity = sizeof(void*). Cell count = some small number (8 is plenty). The slot store's existing per-slot spinlock handles concurrent pushes for free, and pops use the same FIFO ordering the rest of the system already understands.

This is structurally honest about what the system is doing: every box has input slots for its incoming values plus one control slot for its incoming reconfigure messages. All slots, all rings, one mechanism. Reusing the primitive means no new code paths to maintain.

## On knowing which slots to remove

Walk through the algorithm:

The mutator has the OLD box in hand (with its input port names and the slot IDs they map to) and the NEW box just popped from the control queue (with input port names, but no slot IDs filled in yet).

For each port name in NEW: look that name up in OLD's port list.
- **Found in OLD:** copy OLD's slot ID for that name into NEW. The ring buffer keeps all its queued values intact; the new box just keeps reading from the same place.
- **Not found in OLD:** this is a brand-new port. Allocate a fresh slot from the slot store, put the new slot ID into NEW.

For each port name in OLD: look that name up in NEW's port list.
- **Found in NEW:** already handled (shared slot).
- **Not found in NEW:** this slot is abandoned. Add it to the OLD box's "to-be-released" list. When OLD is eventually freed (refcount hits zero), the to-be-released slots get released back to the slot store, and any leftover values in them get logged to the JSONL discard event.

The matching is by port name. Rename = effectively delete + add: if you change "x" to "x_renamed", "x" appears only in OLD (to release) and "x_renamed" appears only in NEW (to allocate). Any queued values on "x" get discarded.

If you wanted "rename keeps the queued values," you'd need an explicit rename declaration in the spec — something like `{"rename": {"old_name": "x", "new_name": "x_renamed"}}`. Worth supporting? Maybe later. For now, rename-as-delete-plus-add is honest about what the message means: the producer who pushed onto "x" probably doesn't know about the rename, so dropping the values is conservative.

## On JSON as the API contract

Sold. JSON it is — for exactly the reason you said: it keeps the language bridge work as small as possible. Every language can format JSON in a few lines. Asking each language bridge to know about C struct layouts would be a big lift, and would discourage adding new languages.

So the API shape becomes:

```
runtime_apply_box_spec(target_id, json_bytes, json_len, error_out)
```

The runtime, in the caller's thread (whichever language called it), parses the JSON into a new box struct using the same parser the graph loader uses today (the refactored version that takes an in-memory buffer instead of a file path). Validates. Pushes the struct pointer to the target's control slot. Returns success or a parse error.

The mutator, when it eventually wins the gather flag, just pops the pointer and swaps. Pure pointer work.

For the JSONL transcript, we can keep the raw JSON bytes around long enough to log them. Either: copy the JSON into the JSONL event at push time (so the log records "this is the spec that was queued"), or re-serialize the box struct back to JSON at apply time (so the log records "this is what actually applied"). I'd vote the latter — it's the source of truth for what the box became, not just what was requested.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **Reusing the slot store for control messages is a design coherence win.** Every "thing that moves through the system" goes through the same ring-buffer primitive — input values, output values, now reconfigure messages. One mechanism, one set of edge cases to learn, one set of tests to maintain. The alternative would have been a special "control plane" mechanism just for reconfigure; that's exactly the kind of code that drifts away from the main code over time and grows its own subtle bugs.
- **The port-name set-difference algorithm is what graphdiff libraries do.** When git compares two file trees, or when a UI framework diffs old-tree-of-elements vs new-tree-of-elements, the algorithm is identical to what we just sketched: match by stable identifier, ports-only-in-old get destroyed, ports-only-in-new get created, ports-in-both get preserved. You've reinvented React's reconciliation algorithm at the level of a single box's input ports.
- **"JSON as the API contract" is the contract-as-text-not-binary principle.** Same reasoning that makes HTTP win over RPC frameworks for cross-system APIs: anyone with a string formatter can participate, no SDK required. By keeping the runtime API at the JSON layer, you make "add a new language to SoraMech" a smaller commitment — the new language only needs to learn JSON, which it almost certainly already knows.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Okay actually I have questions - when you say API what do you mean? Are we still passing the reconfigure json to the input ports? How does that work?

## Assistant

Good question — I've been sloppy with "API." Let me clarify the dataflow, because there are actually two different channels involved.

## Two distinct channels

**Channel 1 — wires (the dataflow plane):** boxes connected to other boxes by connections. Producer fires, pushes its output bytes into the consumer's input slot's ring buffer. The consumer's worker pops from the ring. This is what you've been thinking of as "input ports."

**Channel 2 — direct function calls into the runtime (the control plane):** any code running inside a box's invoke can call C functions like `runtime_create_box(...)`, `runtime_connect(...)`, and now `runtime_apply_box_spec(...)`. These are NOT wire-based — they're plain function calls that the language bridge exposes to box code.

When I said "API," I meant **Channel 2.** The reconfigure JSON does NOT ride the wire system to reach the target box. It travels via a direct function call from whichever box wants to do the reconfiguring.

## Walking through a concrete example

Suppose you have a Lua box called `planner` that wants to reconfigure a box called `worker`. The planner's Lua function looks something like:

```lua
function planner.run(some_input)
    -- decide we want to change how worker behaves
    local new_spec = {
        kind = "call",
        lang = "lua",
        ref  = "src/worker.lua",
        fn   = "new_implementation",
        inputs = { { name = "x", type = "string" } }
    }
    soramech.apply_box("worker", new_spec)
    return some_output
end
```

When `planner` fires, its Lua function calls `soramech.apply_box("worker", new_spec)`. That's the language bridge. Inside the bridge:

1. It serializes `new_spec` (a Lua table) to JSON bytes.
2. It calls the C function `runtime_apply_box_spec("worker", json_bytes, json_len, &err)`.
3. That C function parses the JSON, builds a fresh `box_t` struct in memory, and pushes the struct's pointer onto **worker's control queue** (the per-box slot we talked about).

The reconfigure JSON never touched a wire. It came into existence inside planner's invoke, got passed through the bridge as a function argument, and ended up as a struct pointer in worker's control queue.

## Why the control queue and not an input port?

Two reasons:

**First, control messages aren't input values.** An input value on port "x" is a piece of data that the box's function consumes. A reconfigure message is metadata about the box's identity. They're different in kind, and mixing them on the same channel led to all the peek-vs-pop race conditions we struggled with for a while.

**Second, the producer of the reconfigure isn't a graph-wired box.** It's whatever code happens to be running that decided to reconfigure something. Could be a Lua box's invoke. Could be a C function called from anywhere. Could even be a future REST endpoint someone wires up to let an external system reconfigure boxes. None of these are connected to the target box by a wire — they all just call the same C function.

## What about the "reconfigure box kind" we discussed early on?

That idea — a box whose JOB is to receive a JSON spec on an input port and apply it as a reconfigure — still works in this design, but it's no longer a special box kind. It's just a normal box with a regular function that happens to call `soramech.apply_box(...)` internally.

So if you DO want reconfigure messages to flow through your map's wire system (e.g., one box produces a spec, another consumes and applies it), you'd build it like this:

```
[spec_producer] --wire (carries JSON)--> [reconfigure_relay_box]
                                              |
                                              | (its invoke calls
                                              |  soramech.apply_box)
                                              v
                                         (control queue of "worker")
```

The wire carries the JSON to `reconfigure_relay_box`. That box's function reads the JSON from its input port and calls the runtime API. The runtime API pushes the struct pointer to worker's control queue.

The wire is still there for users who want the dataflow style. But the runtime mechanism underneath is the same direct function call.

## What the control queue actually receives

The control queue stores **already-parsed box-struct pointers**, not raw JSON. The parsing happens in the caller's thread (wherever `runtime_apply_box_spec` was called from), so the mutator on the target box doesn't have to do any JSON work — it just pops a pointer and swaps.

If multiple reconfigures are pushed in quick succession (say, planner_a and planner_b both call `soramech.apply_box("worker", ...)` at nearly the same time), both struct pointers land in worker's control queue. The mutator processes them in FIFO order — first one wins the swap, second one swaps over the first, etc.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The "control plane vs data plane" distinction is the same one networking made decades ago.** TCP carries data; ICMP carries control. They share the wire but are conceptually separate, and confusing them creates exactly the kind of edge case we wrestled with for several rounds of design. Your runtime made the same split: input slots carry data, control queue carries control, runtime API is the function-call entry point that bridges between "code running in a box" and "the control plane mechanism." Once the split is named, the design clarifies.
- **"API" in this codebase always means C functions callable from any thread, not wire-based message passing.** Same as create_box and connect today. The convention is: anything that mutates the graph at runtime is a function call, not a dataflow. Dataflow is for values; function calls are for graph changes. Keeping these orthogonal means box authors don't have to invent ad-hoc protocols for "how do I tell the runtime to add a wire" — they just call a function.
- **The relay box pattern is the bridge between wires and the runtime API.** If you want a dataflow-style reconfigure, you build a normal box whose function happens to call the runtime API on each fire. The runtime doesn't know or care; it just sees another invocation of `runtime_apply_box_spec`. This means "dataflow reconfigure" is composable — you can have multiple producers feeding one relay box, or one producer feeding multiple relay boxes, all expressible with normal wires.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Does this clear up where the JSON travels and what "API" means? Once you're comfortable with this picture, the implementation tasks should make more sense — task #15 (the API entry point) and task #18 (the language bridges) are the two pieces of the function-call path; task #17 (control queue) is the per-box slot that receives the resulting pointers; tasks #10/#12/#13/#14 are the gather/mutator mechanics that read from the control queue.

## Assistant

Good clarifying questions — let me untangle the layers.

## Where the code lives

The function `soramech.apply_box` is registered by the **language bridge**, not added as a file in libs/. It's the same shape as the existing `soramech.create_box` and `soramech.connect` — those aren't libs/ files either; they're functions the Lua spec exposes to every Lua box.

To make this concrete: when the Lua spec initialises a worker thread, it pushes a table called `soramech` into the global Lua state. The table has entries for create_box, connect, and (after 320) apply_box. Any Lua box's function can call any of these without importing anything — they're just available.

So the answer to "are we adding a file to libs/?" is no, not for the foundational mechanism. **The mechanism is bridge code.** libs/ is reserved for higher-level helpers — for example, if a pattern emerges where users keep building call-box specs by hand (`{kind="call", lang="lua", ref="...", fn="...", inputs={...}}`), we could add `libs/box-builder.lua` that exposes `box.call("src/foo.lua", "process", {"x", "y"})` and returns a populated spec table. That's optional polish that lands when there's a clear use case, not part of 320.

## How a box reconfigures a different box

Inside a box's function (in any language), the box code calls `soramech.apply_box(target_id, spec)`. The target_id is just a string — the id of whichever box you want to reconfigure.

Where does the target_id come from? Three patterns, all valid:

**Pattern A — hardcoded in the box's source.**
```lua
function controller.run(input)
    soramech.apply_box("worker", { kind="call", fn="new_fn", ... })
    return input
end
```
The literal string `"worker"` is in the source. Fine when the controller box always reconfigures the same target. Simple, no extra wires needed.

**Pattern B — supplied as an input value.**
```lua
function controller.run(target_id, spec_json)
    soramech.apply_box(target_id, spec_json)
end
```
The controller has two input ports: target_id and spec_json. Upstream boxes push values onto these ports. When controller fires, its function reads the values and calls apply_box. Useful when the target depends on data.

**Pattern C — id captured from a previous create_box call.**
```lua
function planner.run(seed)
    local new_id = soramech.create_box({ kind="call", ... })
    -- ...later...
    soramech.apply_box(new_id, { kind="call", fn="updated_fn", ... })
end
```
The id was generated when the box was created. The planner remembers it and uses it later for reconfigure. This is "I'm managing a fleet of boxes I created" territory.

All three patterns work with the same mechanism. The runtime doesn't care how the controller decided which target to talk to — it just gets a string and a spec.

## Thread-pool interaction, step by step

This is the bit that's most worth walking through carefully, because the timing has some non-obvious moments.

**Step 1 — Controller fires.** Upstream boxes push values into controller's input slots. The dispatch spawns a task for controller. The pool picks a worker, say W1. W1 enters `dispatch_action` for controller.

**Step 2 — Controller's invoke runs on W1.** W1 holds controller's gather flag briefly during the input gather, then releases the flag and calls the Lua spec's invoke. The invoke runs controller's function. Inside the function: `soramech.apply_box("worker", spec_table)` is called.

**Step 3 — apply_box runs on the SAME thread (W1).** The Lua bridge serialises the spec_table to JSON. It calls the C function `runtime_apply_box_spec("worker", json, len, &err)`. The C function parses the JSON, allocates a new box struct, fills in the fields. All of this happens on W1.

**Step 4 — The new struct's pointer is pushed onto worker's control queue.** This is a slot operation, thread-safe via the slot's spinlock. The push is fire-and-forget — it doesn't wait for any worker on the target. If worker's control queue is full (8 cells already taken), W1 briefly waits for space. In practice it's always empty.

**Step 5 — apply_box returns.** Control returns to controller's Lua function, which returns its output value. W1 finishes controller's fire — pushes controller's output to downstream slots, decrements controller's refcount, etc. W1 is done with controller and goes back to the pool to pick up another task.

**Step 6 — Eventually a worker arrives at the target box.** This is the decoupled-in-time part. Some time later — could be microseconds, could be longer — a worker (say W2, could be the same as W1 or different) arrives at worker box. W2 enters dispatch_action, tries to acquire worker's gather flag, succeeds, checks worker's control queue, sees the pending pointer. W2 becomes the mutator: pops the pointer, resolves slot mappings, atomically swaps the graph slot, releases the gather flag, yarqs.

**Step 7 — The reconfigure has landed.** From now on, anyone looking up "worker" via the graph slot gets the new box struct. Subsequent fires of worker run under the new shape.

## What triggers W2 to arrive at the target?

This is the subtle bit. The thread pool doesn't proactively schedule work for the target box just because a reconfigure was pushed to its control queue. The pool only schedules tasks for boxes whose inputs are ready (or boxes that were explicitly spawned via dispatch_spawn).

So if no producer pushes values to worker's inputs after the reconfigure, no worker arrives at worker, and the reconfigure sits unread in the control queue forever. This is fine semantically — reconfigure means "next time you fire, fire as this new shape." If worker never fires, the reconfigure is moot.

**But** if you want the reconfigure to take effect EAGERLY — even when no inputs are queued — the runtime API should automatically dispatch_spawn the target after pushing to its control queue. The spawned task arrives at worker, finds the control queue non-empty, becomes mutator (the mutator path doesn't need any input values — it just does the swap), applies the reconfigure, yarqs.

My proposal: **apply_box automatically spawns a task for the target.** Cost is one task in the pool queue (cheap). Benefit is eager application — the reconfigure lands quickly even if no producer is pushing to the target. Workers that arrive at the target but find nothing to do beyond the reconfigure just process the reconfigure and exit. No fire happens unless inputs are also available.

## Putting it all together — the dataflow picture

```
upstream values ──wire──> controller's input slots
                              │
                              ▼
                       (W1 fires controller)
                              │
                       controller.run() called
                              │
                       calls soramech.apply_box("worker", spec)
                              │
                       (still W1) parse JSON, build struct,
                       push struct pointer onto:
                              │
                              ▼
                       worker's CONTROL QUEUE (not its inputs!)
                              │
                       apply_box returns
                              │
                       controller.run() returns its output
                              │
                       (W1 done with controller)

         ...later, separately...

                       (W2 arrives at worker, e.g. because a
                        producer pushed an input, or because
                        apply_box auto-spawned a task)
                              │
                       W2 acquires worker's gather flag
                              │
                       W2 checks worker's control queue → non-empty
                              │
                       W2 becomes mutator
                              │
                       atomic graph slot swap: worker → new struct
                              │
                       W2 yarqs
                              │
                       (next worker to visit "worker" runs under new shape)
```

The wire system (upstream → controller) is the data plane. The function call (controller → apply_box → control queue) is the control plane. The mutator path (W2 → graph slot swap) is the application. Three distinct phases, decoupled in time, each one running on whatever thread the pool happened to assign.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The "language bridge exposes runtime APIs" pattern is a thin layer with deep consequences.** Boxes get to participate in graph modification without knowing anything about graph internals — they just call functions. The bridge is the only piece that knows both the language's calling conventions AND the C function signatures. New languages added later only need to learn one shape: "expose these N functions in your soramech namespace." Everything downstream stays the same.
- **The "decoupled in time" property of pushing to the control queue is what makes this safe to call from anywhere.** The caller doesn't have to know whether the target is currently busy, whether other reconfigures are pending, whether the target even has inputs queued. The push is fire-and-forget; the application happens when it can. Same pattern as posting to a message queue in a distributed system — the producer never blocks on the consumer's state.
- **Auto-spawn after apply_box is the small piece of glue that makes the API feel responsive.** Without it, reconfigures could land arbitrarily late, depending on when the next input arrives at the target. With it, the runtime guarantees the reconfigure is processed within "one trip through the pool queue" — typically microseconds. The cost is one extra task in the queue; the benefit is predictable timing for users.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Question back

Do you want **apply_box to auto-spawn a task on the target after pushing to the control queue**, so reconfigures land eagerly even when no inputs are queued? Or do you prefer the lazy "applies whenever the target next fires" semantic? My instinct is auto-spawn — feels right for an API that users will reach for — but it's your call.

And: should `soramech.apply_box` return immediately after pushing (fire-and-forget), or should it return some kind of "confirmation that the reconfigure landed" — a way for the caller to wait for the swap to happen? Fire-and-forget is simpler; the confirmation pattern would require a callback or a future, which is more complex but might be useful for "I want to know my reconfigure landed before I continue."

You're right and I was off in the weeds. Let me recalibrate, because I had the wrong mental model and was steering toward a design that doesn't match your architectural intent.

## What I got wrong

I was treating "the runtime API" as a thing users would call from their language code — like Java's reflection API, where user code reaches into the runtime to manipulate it. That's the function-call-as-a-library pattern, and you're right that it pulls in the wrong direction: it makes SoraMech-the-system depend on being importable from arbitrary user code, which is exactly the inversion you don't want.

Your model is the opposite: **users express what they want by building boxes and wires, and the runtime interprets that.** If you want to reconfigure a box, you don't write code that calls reconfigure — you put a reconfigure-utility-box in your map, wire its output to the target, and let the dataflow do the work. The runtime never gets called as a library; it's always the substrate.

The phase 3 progress file confirms this — 319f's `BOX_CREATE_BOX` and `BOX_CONNECT` are already dispatch primitives (box kinds), language-agnostic. The Lua bridge functions from 319d still exist but they're the legacy path; the box-kind pattern is the project's current direction. I should have spotted this and stayed consistent with it.

## The corrected architecture

Let me re-walk what we're actually building, all the way through:

**The utility-box (renaming from "create-a-box"):** a dispatch primitive box kind that handles create / reconfigure / delete based on the spec it receives. It has:
- One input port — the JSON spec
- One output wire — points to the target

When the utility-box fires, it doesn't produce a normal value. Instead, it parses the JSON into a fully-built box struct (in the firing worker's thread), then pushes a **pointer to that struct** onto its output wire's destination — which is one of the target box's input slots. The push is tagged so the target's gather knows "this isn't a value; this is a reconfigure."

**The wire system carries everything:** values, reconfigure messages, anything. The same slot, same ring buffer infrastructure. The ordering ring tag distinguishes what's in each cell. The user builds reconfigure topologies with the same wires they use for everything else, visible in the editor, expressible in JSON.

**The target box's gather is atomic.** When a worker arrives at the target, it tries to CAS the box's gather flag from 0 to 1. Wins → enters gather, peeks each port's ordering ring (safe because nobody else can be in gather right now). If any port shows a RECONFIGURE tag, the worker becomes the mutator: pops the reconfigure messages, swaps the graph slot to the new box, releases the gather flag, yarqs. If all ports show value tags, the worker pops values normally and fires.

**The race I was so worried about doesn't happen** because the gather lock is the serialization point. Two workers can't both be in gather at the same time on the same box, so peek-then-pop is honest within the lock.

**No separate control queue.** The reconfigure message lives in the same slot the values do. The ring buffer's ordering tag is what carries the kind discrimination.

## Walking through a fire-then-reconfigure scenario

```
upstream JSON producer ──wire──> utility_box ──wire──> target_box (input "x")
                                                  │
                                                  ▼
                                         pushes a struct pointer
                                         with RING_RECONFIGURE tag
                                         onto target_box's input "x"
```

When utility_box fires:
1. It pops a JSON spec from its input port.
2. It parses the JSON into a `box_t` struct (in the worker's thread).
3. It pushes the struct pointer onto its output wire's target — which lands in target_box's input slot for port "x", tagged as RECONFIGURE.

Some time later, when target_box is fired:
4. A worker arrives at target_box, CASes the gather flag.
5. Inside gather: peeks each input port's ordering ring. Port "x" shows RECONFIGURE at its head.
6. Worker becomes mutator. Pops the struct pointer from port "x". Resolves slot mappings between OLD and NEW (port-name set-diff). Atomically swaps target_box's graph slot from OLD to NEW. Releases gather flag. Yarqs.
7. Next worker to fire target_box gets NEW via the graph slot. NEW's input slots are the same physical slots (matched by port name), so any queued values on the unaffected ports are still there.

## Why this is simpler than what I was proposing

- **No new infrastructure.** No separate control queue, no per-box control slot. Just the existing input slots with an extra ordering tag value.
- **Visible in the editor.** A reconfigure wire IS a wire. Users see it on the canvas, drag it like any other wire.
- **Composable with existing patterns.** A read box can feed a static spec to a utility-box. A planner box can generate dynamic specs. A fan-in topology can feed multiple producers' specs to one utility-box.
- **No language bridge bloat.** Languages don't expose runtime mutation as library functions. They only have to know how to fire box functions and serialise their outputs.

## What this changes about the issue files

The 320 issue I just wrote is wrong — it's based on the control-queue + bridge-function design. I need to rewrite it to reflect the box-kind + per-port-third-ring approach. Tasks #15 (runtime API entry point) and #18 (language bridges) should be replaced with tasks for the BOX_RECONFIGURE (or unified utility) box kind and the per-port RECONFIGURE tag handling. Task #17 (control slot) goes away.

Before I rewrite, let me ask the right questions this time:

## Questions back

1. **Naming the box kind.** You floated "utility-box" — is that what you want? It's a fairly broad name; a more specific alternative would be "spec-applier" or "reconfigure-box" or just "box-control." If utility-box is going to absorb everything (create, reconfigure, delete), the broad name fits. If it's specifically for reconfigure-style operations with create and delete as separate kinds, a narrower name is better. My instinct: **utility-box** with the spec's `action` field deciding what it does (create / reconfigure / delete based on whether target exists and what the spec asks for). One box kind, three meanings, dispatched by content.

2. **What does "target" mean — the box at the wire's other end?** I assumed the utility-box's output wire goes to a specific input slot of the target box, and the target is identified by being at the wire's other end. Is that right? The alternative is the utility-box has TWO inputs — one for the JSON spec, one for the target_id string — and its output goes... nowhere? Or it has no output? My read of your description is: the wire end IS the target, no separate target-id field needed.

3. **Does 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX get folded into the new utility-box, or stay separate?** They're the same operation if utility-box handles create. Folding means one box kind for all graph mutation; staying separate means we have BOX_CREATE_BOX (existing) + BOX_RECONFIGURE (new) + later BOX_DELETE (or fold delete into reconfigure). My instinct: fold them all. One mutation primitive, one mental model.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **"The runtime is the substrate; the user expresses through it" is a clean architectural inversion.** Most systems put themselves in the import path; users have to learn the system's API to use it. SoraMech goes the other way: the user builds boxes and wires; the runtime makes sense of them. This is the same inversion that visual programming languages (Scratch, Max/MSP, Pure Data) made decades ago — the IDE isn't a window into the code, it IS the code. Sticking to this discipline matters because every concession to the "import us as a library" pattern weakens the visual-programming nature of the system.
- **The per-port third ring really was the right design** — the gather lock makes the peek-pop race I was worried about disappear. I was solving a problem that didn't exist within the architecture you laid out. The control queue solution I proposed was elegant in isolation, but it was a layer-mismatch: I was trying to keep "control" out of the data plane when your design explicitly wants them in the same plane, distinguished by ordering tag rather than by channel. The reason it's safe to mix them is the gather lock, not channel separation.
- **319f already proved the box-kind pattern works for graph mutation.** I should have built directly on that precedent rather than pulling in a different shape. When a project has an established mutation primitive pattern (BOX_CREATE_BOX as a dispatch kind), the new mutation primitive should match — same shape, same expectations, same composability with the editor. Consistency at this level pays dividends in user mental load.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

## Assistant

Good news: 319f's existing pattern matches what we want for create. The hard part is unifying the three operations cleanly. Let me lay out what I see and ask one specific question.

## How 319f's BOX_CREATE_BOX works today

Reading the code: a BOX_CREATE_BOX has one input port (`spec`) carrying JSON bytes. When fired, it parses the JSON, allocates a new box at a fresh graph slot, and emits the new box's id as a string on its output wire. Downstream consumers receive the id as a normal string value and can use it (e.g., a BOX_CONNECT downstream takes the id and wires the new box to something).

The key thing: BOX_CREATE_BOX's output wire carries the NEW box's id, not control traffic. The output is just a normal string.

## What changes with the unification

For reconfigure, the user's model is different: the utility-box's output wire points AT the target box's input port, and the message that flows on the wire is a control-tagged pointer (the new box struct) rather than a normal value. The target's gather sees the RECONFIGURE tag and handles it.

For delete, same shape: a delete-marker flows on the output wire to the target, with a DELETE tag.

So the three operations have **different output semantics**:

- **Create:** output is a normal string value (the new id); wire goes wherever a consumer wants the id.
- **Reconfigure:** output is a RECONFIGURE-tagged pointer; wire MUST go to the target box that's being reconfigured.
- **Delete:** output is a DELETE-tagged marker; wire MUST go to the target box that's being deleted.

The utility-box, on each fire, has to decide which of these three to do. Two ways to decide:

**Option A — decide by the spec's `action` field.**
```json
{ "action": "create", "spec": { ...box JSON... } }
{ "action": "reconfigure", "spec": { ...box JSON... } }
{ "action": "delete" }
```
Explicit action field; the utility-box reads it and dispatches. Default action is "create" (matches today's BOX_CREATE_BOX behaviour). The wire endpoint validation depends on the action:
- create: wire endpoint is treated as a normal value consumer of the id string.
- reconfigure / delete: wire endpoint must be the target box; runtime errors if there's a mismatch with anything else.

**Option B — decide by looking up the spec's id field in the graph.**
- If the id exists → it's a reconfigure; push tagged message to target.
- If the id is new → it's a create; emit id string.
- If the spec is empty / has only an action: delete marker → it's a delete; push tagged message to target.

Implicit, content-driven. Cleaner JSON but more "magic."

I lean toward **Option A** — explicit beats implicit, especially for destructive operations like delete. A user who fat-fingers an id and accidentally hits a real box's id would trigger a reconfigure they didn't intend. Explicit `action` makes the operation visible.

## The wire endpoint mismatch problem

For reconfigure and delete, the wire endpoint IS the target. So you might think the spec doesn't need an `id` field — the wire already says where to apply. But there's a wrinkle: what if the user wires a utility-box to box A but the spec's content describes box B?

Three options:
- **Trust the wire, ignore the spec's id.** Wire endpoint = target, full stop. Spec's id field is informational only (or required to match for safety checks).
- **Trust the spec, ignore the wire endpoint.** Whatever the spec says is the target. Wire endpoint just receives an ack.
- **Both must agree; mismatch is a hard error.** Wire endpoint MUST point at the box the spec describes.

Third option is the safest — it catches mistakes at runtime and forces the user to be consistent. The wire is the visible truth (you can see it in the editor); the spec's id is the textual truth. Requiring them to match means the visual and textual representations stay aligned.

## Question back

**For the unification:** Option A (explicit `action` field) or Option B (content-driven)? And do you want wire-endpoint-must-match-spec-id as a safety check, or is the wire endpoint authoritative (spec's id is just informational)?

My instinct after all this back-and-forth:
- **Option A** for the action field — explicit beats implicit.
- **Wire endpoint is authoritative for reconfigure/delete; spec's id field is optional but if present must match.** This gives the user a single source of truth (the wire) and an optional safety net (the redundant id).
- **Default action is "create"** — matches today's BOX_CREATE_BOX behaviour, so existing maps using BOX_CREATE_BOX continue to work unchanged.

If you sign off on this, I'll rewrite 320 to match. Then I want to ask one more practical question about how the JSON is built (the "users CAN build manually" path you mentioned) so the issue file documents both the supported-but-not-assisted manual path and the intended box-and-wire path.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- **The "explicit action field" pattern shows up everywhere in API design.** REST APIs use HTTP verbs (GET/POST/PUT/DELETE) as explicit actions. SQL uses INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE as explicit operations. Git uses add/commit/reset as explicit verbs. The reason: destructive operations need to be loud. Implicit "the system figures out what you meant" is fine for reads, dangerous for writes. Your utility-box is a write operation, so explicit action is the safe default.
- **Wire endpoint as authoritative ties the visual representation to the runtime behaviour.** A user looking at the editor canvas should be able to see exactly what each utility-box affects by following its output wire. If the spec's id could override the wire, the canvas would be lying. By making the wire authoritative, the canvas is always honest — what you see is what happens.
- **The "matches today's BOX_CREATE_BOX behaviour by default" piece preserves backwards compatibility for free.** Existing maps with create_box-kind boxes (and there are several fixtures already) keep working without any changes, because the default action when the spec doesn't specify one IS create. This is the kind of small consideration that avoids a "we have to update every fixture" migration headache later.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`
